Deformations of functions on surfaces
Sergiy Maksymenko

Abstract
. The paper contains a review on recent progress in the deformational properties of smooth maps from compact surfaces M M to a one-dimensional manifold P P . It covers description of homotopy types of stabilizers and orbits of a large class of smooth functions on surfaces obtained by the author, E. Kudryavtseva, B. Feshchenko, I. Kuznietsova, Yu. Soroka, A. Kravchenko. We also present here a new direct proof of the fact that for generic Morse maps the connected components their orbits are homotopy equivalent to finite products of circles.



2000 Mathematics Subject Classification
37J05, 57S05, 58B05

1.
Introduction

Let
M
be a compact connected surface and
P
be either the real line
{\mathbb{R}}
or the circle
S^{1}
.
For a closed subset
X\subset M
denote by
\mathcal{D}(M,X)
the group of all smooth (
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}
) diffeomorphisms of
M
fixed on
X
.
This group acts from the right on the space
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(M,P)
by the following rule: if
h\in\mathcal{D}(M,X)
and
f\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(M,P)
, then the result of the action of
h
on
f
is the composition map
f\circ h:M\to P
.
For
f\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(M,P)
let
\Sigma_{f}
be the set of its critical points, and

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}(f,X)

\displaystyle=\{h\in\mathcal{D}(M,X)\mid f\circ h=f\},

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}(f,X)

\displaystyle=\{f\circ h\mid h\in\mathcal{D}(M,X)\}

be respectively the
stabilizer
and the
orbit
of
f
under that action.
It will be convenient to say that elements of
\mathcal{S}(f,X)

preserve

f
.
Let also

\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(f,X)=\mathcal{S}(f)\cap\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{id}}(M,X)

be the subgroup of
\mathcal{S}(f)
consisting of isotopic to the identity diffeomorphisms relatively to
X
, though such an isotopy is not required to preserve
f
.
Endow these spaces with
C^{\infty}
topologies and denote by
\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{id}}(M,X)
and
\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{id}}(f,X)
the corresponding path components of
\mathrm{id}_{M}
in
\mathcal{D}(M,X)
and
\mathcal{S}(f,X)
, and by
\mathcal{O}_{f}(f,X)
the path component of
\mathcal{O}(f,X)
containing
f
.
We will omit
X
from notation whenever it is empty.

Let
\mathcal{C}_{\partial}^{\infty}(M,P)\subset\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(M,P)
be the subset consisting of maps
f:M\to P
satisfying the following axiom:

(B)

The map
f
takes a constant value at each connected component of
\partial M
and has no critical points in
\partial M
.

Let also
\mathcal{M}(M,P)\subset\mathcal{C}_{\partial}^{\infty}(M,P)
be the subset consisting of
Morse maps
, i.e. maps having only non-degenerate critical points, i.e. in some local coordinates
(x,y)
at such point
f
is given by the formula
\pm x^{2}\pm y^{2}
for some choice of signs.
Notice that such a polynomial can be characterized as a non-zero homogeneous polynomial of degree
2
having no multiple factors.
A Morse map is called

•

simple
if each connected component of each level set of
f
contains at most one critical point;

•

generic
if it takes distinct value at distinct critical points.

Every generic Morse map is evidently simple.
Denote by
\mathcal{M}^{gen}(M,P)
and
\mathcal{M}^{smp}(M,P)
the sets of all generic and simple Morse maps
M\to P
.
Then

\mathcal{M}^{gen}(M,P)\ \subset\ \mathcal{M}^{smp}(M,P)\ \subset\ \mathcal{M}(M,P).

It is well known that each of these three spaces is
open
and
everywhere dense
in
\mathcal{C}_{\partial}^{\infty}(M,P)
with respect to
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}
topology, e.g.
[
16
, Chapter 6]
.

More generally, let
\mathcal{F}(M,P)\subset\mathcal{C}_{\partial}^{\infty}(M,P)
be the subset consisting of maps satisfying one more axiom:

(L)

For every critical point
z
of
f
, there are local coordinates in which
f
is a homogeneous polynomial
{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\to{\mathbb{R}}
of degree
\geq 2
without multiple factors.

The present paper contains a review of recent results about the homotopy types of
\mathcal{S}(f,X)
and
\mathcal{O}(f,X)
of maps
f\in\mathcal{F}(M,P)
obtained by
S. Maksymenko, B. Feshchenko, A. Kravchenko, I. Kuznietsova, Yu. Soroka,

[
34
,
33
,
35
,
36
,
37
,
39
,
40
,
43
,
44
,
19
,
21
,
20
,
45
,
46
,
47
,
9
,
29
,
31
]
and
E. Kudryavtseva
[
28
,
24
,
23
,
25
,
27
]
,

Notice that we have the following inclusions:

\mathcal{M}(M,P)\ \subset\ \mathcal{F}(M,P)\ \subset\ \mathcal{C}_{\partial}^{\infty}(M,P)\ \subset\ \mathcal{C}^{\infty}(M,P).

It is also easy to show (see §
4.1
) that every
f\in\mathcal{F}(M,P)
has only isolated critical points, so the set of critical points is finite.
Moreover, for every isolated critical point
z
of a
\mathcal{C}^{3}
map
g:{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\to{\mathbb{R}}
the local
topological structure
of level-sets of
g
near
z
is realized by level sets of homogeneous polynomial without multiple factors, see §
4.1
.

Thus
\mathcal{F}(M,P)
consists of ‘‘typical’’ maps with ‘‘toplogically typical’’ critical points, and therefore the presented results thus describe typical deformational properties of smooth maps on surfaces.

2.
Algebraic preliminaries

In this section we will present necessary topological and algebraic definitions and list of preliminary statements.
The reader may skip this section on first reading and use it for the references.
Everywhere in the paper
\lhook\joinrel\rightarrow
will mean a ‘‘
monomorphism
’’,
\rightarrow\!\!\!\!\!\to
an ‘‘
epimorphism
’’, and
\cong
an
isomorphism
.

2.1.
Commutative diagrams

Suppose we are given two sequences of homomorphisms of groups:

\displaystyle\mathbf{u}:K_{1}\xrightarrow{\alpha_{1}}\cdots\xrightarrow{\alpha_{k-1}}K_{k},

\displaystyle\mathbf{v}:L_{1}\xrightarrow{\beta_{1}}\cdots\xrightarrow{\beta_{k-1}}L_{k}.

Then by a
homomorphism

\gamma=(\gamma_{1},\ldots,\gamma_{k}):\mathbf{u}\to\mathbf{v}
we will mean a collection of homomorphisms
\gamma_{i}:K_{i}\to L_{i}
,
i=1,\ldots,k
, making commutative the following diagram:

In this case
\gamma
is said to be an
epimorphism
(resp.
monomorphism
,
isomorphism
) whenever
\gamma_{i}
is so.
In particular, one can talk about
exact sequences
of sequences of homomorphisms.
Also by a
product

\mathbf{u}\times\mathbf{v}
we will mean the following sequence

\mathbf{u}\times\mathbf{v}:K_{1}\times L_{1}\xrightarrow{\alpha_{1}\times\beta_{1}}K_{2}\times L_{2}\xrightarrow{\alpha_{2}\times\beta_{2}}\cdots\xrightarrow{\alpha_{k-1}\times\beta_{k-1}}K_{k}\times L_{k}.

More generally, one can define in an obvious way similar notions for commutative diagrams of arbitrary fixed type not only for chains of homomorphisms.
In particular, by
exact
(3\times 3)
-diagram
we will mean a commutative diagram shown on the left:

in which each row and column is a short exact sequence.
It can be regarded as a
short exact sequence of its rows
\mathbf{u}_{0}\lhook\joinrel\rightarrow\mathbf{u}_{1}\rightarrow\!\!\!\!\!\to\mathbf{u}_{2}
(or columns) being in turn short exact sequences of groups homomorphisms
.
Notice that if all monomorphisms in (
1
) are inclusions of subgroups, then
L
and
A
are normal subgroups of
B
with
K=L\cap A
and that diagrams is isomorphic with the following one

(2)

\displaystyle\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 16.6736pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&&\cr&&&\cr&&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-15.7083pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise via isomorphism being identity on
A
,
L
, and
B
.

2.2.
Sections of homomorphisms

Let
p:G\to Z
be a homomorphism.
Then another homomorphism
s:Z\to G
is called a
section
of
p
, whenever
p\circ s=\mathrm{id}_{Z}
.
In this case
p
must be surjective, and
s
isomorphically maps
Z
onto the subgroup
s(Z)
of
G
.

An essential point here is that
s
must be a
homomorphism
.
For instance
p:{\mathbb{Z}}\xrightarrow{b\bmod n}\!\!\!\!\!\to{\mathbb{Z}}_{n}
for
n\geq 2
has no sections.
Indeed if
s:{\mathbb{Z}}_{n}\to{\mathbb{Z}}
a section, then
s({\mathbb{Z}}_{n})
must be a finite subgroup of
{\mathbb{Z}}
, and therefore it is
\{0\}
.
Thus
s
is the zero homomorphism, and thus
p\circ s=0\not=\mathrm{id}_{{\mathbb{Z}}_{n}}
.

2.3.
Direct products

Let
G
be a group,
G_{1},\ldots,G_{k}
a collection of its subgroups, and
\psi:\mathop{\Pi}\limits_{i=1}^{k}G_{i}\to G
a
map
defined by
\psi(g_{1},\ldots,g_{k})=g_{1}\cdots g_{k}
.
Then
\psi
is an
isomorphism of groups
if and only if
G_{1},\ldots,G_{k}
pairwise commute, generate
G
, and
G_{i}\cap G_{j}=\{e\}
for
i\not=j
.
In that case
G

splits into a direct product of its subgroups
G_{1},\ldots,G_{k}

2.4.
Semidirect products

Let
Z
and
A
be two groups with units
e_{Z}
and
e_{A}
respectively, and
\phi:Z\to\mathrm{Aut}(A)
a
homomorphism
into the group of automorphisms of
A
regarded as a group with respect to the composition of automorphisms.
For
x\in Z
and
a\in A
it will be convenient to denote
\phi(x)(a)={}^{x}{a}
, whence for
x,y\in Z
and
a,b\in A
we have that

\displaystyle{}^{(xy)}{a}

\displaystyle=\phi(xy)(a)=\phi(x)\bigl(\phi(y)(a)\bigr)={}^{x}{({}^{y}{a})},

\displaystyle{}^{x}{(ab)}

\displaystyle=\phi(x)(ab)=\phi(x)(a)\cdot\phi(x)(b)={}^{x}{a}\cdot{}^{x}{b}.

Then there is a group structure on the Cartesian product of
sets

A\times Z
, denoted by
A\rtimes_{\phi}Z
and called
a semidirect product of
A
and
Z
(with respect to
\phi
)
, defined by the following rule:

(a,x)\cdot(b,y):=(a\cdot{}^{x}{b},xy),\qquad(a,x),(b,y)\in A\times Z.

If
\phi
is assumed from the context, then
A\rtimes_{\phi}Z
is sometimes denoted simply by
A\rtimes Z
.

One easily check associativity of such multiplication, and that

(a,x)\cdot(b,y)\cdot(c,z):=(a\cdot{}^{x}{b}\cdot{}^{xy}{c},xyz),

the unit is
(e_{A},e_{Z})
, and
(a,x)^{-1}=({}^{x^{-1}}{a^{-1}},x^{-1})
.

It follows that the following maps

\displaystyle\rho:A\to A\rtimes_{\phi}Z,

\displaystyle\rho(a)=(a,e_{Z}),

\displaystyle\sigma:Z\to A\rtimes_{\phi}Z,

\displaystyle\sigma(z)=(e_{A},z),

\displaystyle\pi:A\rtimes_{\phi}Z\to Z,

\displaystyle\pi(a,z)=z,

are homomorphisms,
\rho
isomorphically maps
A
on
A\times e_{Z}
, and
\sigma
is a
section
of
\pi
, i.e.
\pi\circ\sigma=\mathrm{id}_{Z}
, and its isomorphically maps
Z
onto
e_{A}\times Z
.
In other words, we have the following short exact sequence admitting a section:

\textstyle{A\ \ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\rho}
\textstyle{\ A\rtimes Z\ \ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi}
\textstyle{\ Z.\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sigma}

The following statement characterizes semidirect products via short exact sequences admitting sections.

Lemma 2.4.1
.

Let
p:G\to Z
be a homomorphism with
A=\ker(p)
.
Suppose there exists a
section
s:Z\to G
of
p
.
Then
s(Z)
acts on
A
by conjugations, so we get a homomorphism

\phi:Z\to\mathrm{Aut}(A),\qquad\phi(z)(a)=s(z)\cdot a\cdot s(z)^{-1},

and can define the semidirect product
A\rtimes_{\phi}Z
.
Moreover, the map

\psi:A\rtimes_{\phi}Z\to G,\qquad\psi(a,z)=r(a)\sigma(z),

is an
isomorphism
which induces isomorphism of the following short exact sequences:

(3)

\displaystyle\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 11.75pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&&&\cr&&&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-11.75pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise \ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 11.75002pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 2.5.
Semidirect products with
{\mathbb{Z}}

Clearly, any epimorphism
\eta:G\to{\mathbb{Z}}
onto the group
{\mathbb{Z}}
of integers, always has a section: just take any
g\in G
with
\eta(g)=1
, and put
s(n)=g^{n}
.
Then
G
is a semidirect product
\ker(\eta)\rtimes{\mathbb{Z}}
, i.e. a Cartesian product
\ker(\eta)\times{\mathbb{Z}}
of sets with the following multiplication:

(4)

(a,m)(b,n)=\bigl(ag^{m}bg^{-m},\ m+n\bigr).

2.6.
Wreath products

Let
{G}
be a group,
m\geq 1
, and
{G}^{m}
be the
m
-th power of
{G}
, so its elements are
m
-tuples of elements of
{G}
.
Also for
n\geq 1
one can regard the elements of
mn
-power
{G}^{mn}
of
{G}
as
(m\times n)
-matrices whose entries belong to
{G}
.
In particular, there are the following natural
non-effective
actions of
{\mathbb{Z}}
on
{G}^{m}
and
{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}
on
{G}^{mn}
by cyclic shifts of coordinates:

\displaystyle{G}^{m}\times{\mathbb{Z}}\to{G}^{m},

\displaystyle\bigl(\{g_{i}\}_{i=0}^{m-1},a\bigr)\mapsto\{g_{i+a}\},

\displaystyle{G}^{mn}\times{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}\to{G}^{mn},

\displaystyle\bigl(\{g_{i,j}\}_{i=0,\ldots,m-1}^{j=0,\ldots,n-1},(a,b)\bigr)\mapsto\{g_{i+a,j+b}\},

for
a,b\in{\mathbb{Z}}
, where
i+a
is taken modulo
m
, and
j+b
is taken modulo
n
.
These actions reduce to
effective
actions of
{\mathbb{Z}}_{m}
on
{G}^{m}
and
{\mathbb{Z}}_{m}\times{\mathbb{Z}}_{n}
on
{G}^{mn}
.
Let

\displaystyle G\wr_{m}{{\mathbb{Z}}},

\displaystyle G\wr{{\mathbb{Z}}_{m}},

\displaystyle G\wr_{mn}{{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}},

\displaystyle G\wr{({\mathbb{Z}}_{m}\times{\mathbb{Z}}_{n})}

be the respective semidirect products induced by the above actions.
Such semidirect products are called
wreath products
.
For instance,
G\wr_{m}{{\mathbb{Z}}}
is a cartesian product of sets
G^{m}\times{\mathbb{Z}}
with the following operation:

(g_{0},\ldots,g_{m-1},a)\cdot(h_{0},\ldots,h_{m-1},b)=\\
=\bigl(g_{0}h_{a},\,g_{1}h_{a+1},\,\ldots,\,g_{m-1}h_{a-1},\,a+b\bigr),

where all indices are taken modulo
m
.
The multiplications in other groups
G\wr{{\mathbb{Z}}_{m}}
,
G\wr_{mn}{{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}}
, and
G\wr{({\mathbb{Z}}_{m}\times{\mathbb{Z}}_{n})}
are similar.

These groups will play the key role in what follows.
For a group
G
denote by
Z(G)
its center and by
G^{\prime}:=[G,G]
its derived subgroup (commutant).
Let also
\mathrm{ab}:G\to G/G^{\prime}
be the natural abelianization epimorphism.

Lemma 2.6.1
.

[
31
]

Let
G
be any group and
m,n\geq 1
.
Then

\displaystyle Z\bigl(G\wr_{m}{{\mathbb{Z}}}\bigr)=\bigl\{(g,\ldots,g,mk)\mid g\in G,k\in{\mathbb{Z}}\bigr\}\ \cong\ Z(G)\times{\mathbb{Z}},

\displaystyle Z\bigl(G\wr_{m,n}{{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}}\bigr)=\bigl\{(g,\ldots,g,mk,nl)\mid g\in G,k,l\in{\mathbb{Z}}\bigr\}\ \cong\ Z(G)\times{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}.

Moreover, the following maps

\displaystyle\gamma:G\wr_{m}{{\mathbb{Z}}}\to(G/G^{\prime})\times{\mathbb{Z}},

\displaystyle\gamma(g_{1},\ldots,g_{n},k)=\bigl(\mathrm{ab}(g_{1}\cdots g_{m}),k),

\displaystyle\delta:G\wr_{m,n}{{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}}\to(G/G^{\prime})\times{\mathbb{Z}}^{2},

\displaystyle\!\gamma(\{g_{i,j}\},k,l)=\Bigl(\mathrm{ab}\bigl(\mathop{\Pi}_{i=1}^{m}\mathop{\Pi}_{j=1}^{n}g_{i,j}\bigr),k,l\Bigr)

are well-defined surjective homomorphisms with

\displaystyle\bigl(G\wr_{m}{{\mathbb{Z}}}\bigr)^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\ker(\gamma)=\bigl\{(g_{1},\ldots,g_{n},0)\mid\mathop{\Pi}_{i=1}^{m}g_{i}\in G^{\prime}\bigr\},

\displaystyle\bigl(G\wr_{m,n}{{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}}\bigr)^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\ker(\delta)=\bigl\{(\{g_{i,j}\},0,0)\mid\mathop{\Pi}_{i=1}^{m}\mathop{\Pi}_{j=1}^{n}g_{i,j}\in G^{\prime}\bigr\},

so we have the following commutative diagrams:

\textstyle{G\wr_{m}{{\mathbb{Z}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{ab}}
\textstyle{\frac{G\wr_{m}{{\mathbb{Z}}}}{\bigl(G\wr_{m}{{\mathbb{Z}}}\bigr)^{\prime}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\eta}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{G\wr_{m}{{\mathbb{Z}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\gamma}
\textstyle{G/G^{\prime}\times{\mathbb{Z}}}

\textstyle{G\wr_{m,n}{{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{ab}}
\textstyle{\frac{G\wr_{m,n}{{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}}}{\bigl(G\wr_{m,n}{{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}}\bigr)^{\prime}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\nu}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{G\wr_{m,n}{{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\delta}
\textstyle{G/G^{\prime}\times{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}}

for unique isomorphisms
\eta
and
\nu
.

Finally, if
G
is
torsion free
, then so are
G\wr_{m}{{\mathbb{Z}}}
and
G\mathop{\wr}\limits_{m,n}{\mathbb{Z}}
.

Notes to the proof.

Statements about centers, derived subgroups, and abelianization are proved in I. Kuznietsova and Yu. Soroka
[
31
]
.
The latter statement about torsion free property of
G\wr_{m}{{\mathbb{Z}}}
is established in
[
44
, Lemma 2.2]
, and the same arguments can be used to prove it for
G\wr_{m,n}{{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}}
.
∎

2.7.
Special short exact sequences

In what follows for
m,n\geq 1
we will use the following short exact sequences:

(5)

\displaystyle\mathbf{z}_{0}

\displaystyle:\ \{1\}\lhook\joinrel\rightarrow\{1\}\rightarrow\!\!\!\!\!\to\{1\},

(6)

\displaystyle\mathbf{z}_{1}

\displaystyle:\ {\mathbb{Z}}\lhook\joinrel\xrightarrow{\penalty\ \mathrm{id}\penalty\ }{\mathbb{Z}}\rightarrow\!\!\!\!\!\to\{1\},

(7)

\displaystyle\mathbf{z}_{m}

\displaystyle:\ m{\mathbb{Z}}\lhook\joinrel\xrightarrow{\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ }{\mathbb{Z}}\xrightarrow{\penalty\ \bmod m\penalty\ }\!\!\!\!\!\to{\mathbb{Z}}_{m},

(8)

\displaystyle\mathbf{z}_{m,n}

\displaystyle:\ m{\mathbb{Z}}\times n{\mathbb{Z}}\lhook\joinrel\xrightarrow{\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ }{\mathbb{Z}}\times{\mathbb{Z}}\xrightarrow{\penalty\ (\mathrm{mod}\,m,\ \mathrm{mod}\,n)\penalty\ }\!\!\!\!\!\to{\mathbb{Z}}_{m}\times{\mathbb{Z}}_{n},

Then for a short exact sequence
\mathbf{q}:K\lhook\joinrel\xrightarrow{\penalty\ \alpha\penalty\ }L\xrightarrow{\penalty\ \beta\penalty\ }\!\!\!\!\!\to M
we have the following two exact
(3\times 3)
-diagram:

(9)

0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 138.2808pt\raise-46.62671pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ {\mathbb{Z}}^{2}\ \ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 206.25883pt\raise-46.62671pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern-3.0pt\lower 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 206.25883pt\raise-46.62671pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ {\mathbb{Z}}_{m}\times{\mathbb{Z}}_{n}\ }$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces$}}

where
p^{\prime}
and
p^{\prime\prime}
are the projection to the last coordinates, and

\displaystyle\alpha^{\prime}\bigl(a_{1},\ldots,a_{m},mk\bigr)

\displaystyle=\bigl(\alpha(a_{1}),\ldots,\alpha(a_{m}),mk\bigr),

\displaystyle\beta^{\prime}\bigl(b_{1},\ldots,b_{m},k\bigr)

\displaystyle=\bigl(\beta(b_{1}),\ldots,\beta(b_{m}),k\bmod m\bigr)

\displaystyle\alpha^{\prime\prime}\bigl(\{a_{i,j}\},mk,nl\bigr)

\displaystyle=\bigl(\{\alpha(a_{i,j})\},mk,nl\bigr),

\displaystyle\beta^{\prime\prime}\bigl(\{b_{i,j}\},k,l\bigr)

\displaystyle=\bigl(\{\beta(b_{i,j})\},k\bmod m,l\bmod n\bigr),

\alpha_{i}\in K
,
\beta_{i}\in L
for all
i=1,\ldots,m
,
j=1,\ldots,n
, and
k,l\in{\mathbb{Z}}
.
The middle horizontal sequences of both diagrams will be called the
wreath product of
\mathbf{q}
with
\mathbf{z}_{m}
and
\mathbf{z}_{m,n}
and denoted by
\mathbf{q}\wr\mathbf{z}_{m}
and
\mathbf{q}\wr\mathbf{z}_{m,n}^{2}
respectively.
Thus (
9
) and (
10
) can be written as short exact sequences of their rows:

(11)

\displaystyle\mathbf{q}^{m}\lhook\joinrel\rightarrow\mathbf{q}\wr\mathbf{z}_{m}\rightarrow\!\!\!\!\!\to\mathbf{z}_{m},

\displaystyle\mathbf{q}^{mn}\lhook\joinrel\rightarrow\mathbf{q}\wr\mathbf{z}_{m,n}\rightarrow\!\!\!\!\!\to\mathbf{z}_{m,n}

Evidently,

\displaystyle\mathbf{q}\wr\mathbf{z}_{m,1}^{2}\ \cong\ \mathbf{q}\wr\mathbf{z}_{1,m}^{2},

\displaystyle\mathbf{q}\wr\mathbf{z}_{1}\ \cong\ \mathbf{q}\times\mathbf{z}_{1}:\ \ K\times{\mathbb{Z}}\ \lhook\joinrel\rightarrow\ L\mathop{\wr}\limits_{1}{\mathbb{Z}}\equiv L\times{\mathbb{Z}}\ \rightarrow\!\!\!\!\!\to\ M,

\displaystyle\mathbf{z}_{k}\wr\mathbf{z}_{m}:\ \ (k{\mathbb{Z}})^{m}\times m{\mathbb{Z}}\ \lhook\joinrel\xrightarrow{\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ }\ {\mathbb{Z}}\mathop{\wr}\limits_{m}{\mathbb{Z}}\ \xrightarrow{\penalty\ }\!\!\!\!\!\to\ {\mathbb{Z}}_{k}\wr{\mathbb{Z}}_{m},

\displaystyle\mathbf{z}_{k}\wr\mathbf{z}_{m,n}^{2}:\ (k{\mathbb{Z}})^{mn}\times m{\mathbb{Z}}\times n{\mathbb{Z}}\ \lhook\joinrel\xrightarrow{\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ }\ {\mathbb{Z}}\mathop{\wr}\limits_{m,n}{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}\ \xrightarrow{\penalty\ }\!\!\!\!\!\to\ {\mathbb{Z}}_{k}\wr({\mathbb{Z}}_{m}\times{\mathbb{Z}}_{n}).

2.8.
Garside elements

Let
G
be any group with unit
e
and
m\geq 1
.
Then the element
\gamma=(\underbrace{e,\ldots,e}_{m},m)\in G\wr_{m}{{\mathbb{Z}}}
will be called the
Garside
element.
By Lemma
2.6.1

\gamma
belongs to the center of
G\wr_{m}{{\mathbb{Z}}}
.

2.8.1.
Garside sequences

Let
\mathbf{q}:K\lhook\joinrel\rightarrow L\rightarrow\!\!\!\!\!\to M
be a short exact sequence and
m\geq 1
.
For simplicity assume that
K
is a subgroup of
L
and let
e
be the common unit element of
K
and
L
.
Consider the wreath product

(12)

\mathbf{q}\wr\mathbf{z}_{m}:K^{m}\times m{\mathbb{Z}}\lhook\joinrel\rightarrow L\mathop{\wr}\limits_{m}{\mathbb{Z}}\rightarrow\!\!\!\!\!\to M\wr{\mathbb{Z}}.

Then the Garside element
\gamma=(e,\ldots,e,m)
of
L\mathop{\wr}\limits_{m}{\mathbb{Z}}
belongs to
K^{m}\times m{\mathbb{Z}}
, and
\gamma^{k}=(e,\ldots,e,km)
for all
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}
.
It follows that there exists the following exact
(3\times 3)
-diagram:

The total sequence
\mathbf{z}_{1}^{2}\lhook\joinrel\rightarrow\mathbf{q}\wr\mathbf{z}_{m,n}^{2}\rightarrow\!\!\!\!\!\to\mathbf{q}\wr\mathbf{z}_{m,n}^{2}
will be called the
Garside sequence of
\mathbf{q}\wr\mathbf{z}_{m,n}^{2}
.

2.8.2.
Diagonal Garside sequences

More generally, let
n\geq 1
, and for each
i=1,\ldots,n
let
\mathbf{q}_{i}:K_{i}\lhook\joinrel\rightarrow L_{i}\rightarrow\!\!\!\!\!\to M_{i}
, be a short exact sequence,
m_{i}\geq 1
,
\gamma_{i}\in L_{i}\mathop{\wr}\limits_{m_{i}}{\mathbb{Z}}
be the corresponding Garside element, and

\widehat{\gamma}=(\gamma_{1},\ldots,\gamma_{n})\in\mathop{\Pi}\limits_{i=1}^{n}(K_{i}^{m_{i}}\times m_{i}{\mathbb{Z}})

be the
‘‘diagonal’’ element
in the product of subgroups
K_{i}^{m_{i}}\times m_{i}{\mathbb{Z}}
generated by the corresponding Garside elements.
Then we have the following commutative diagram:

\mathop{\Pi}\limits_{i=1}^{n}\mathbf{q}_{i}\wr\mathbf{z}_{m_{i}}
.

2.8.3.
Several constructions

For every group
A
one can associate two short exact sequences
\mathsf{s}(A):A=A\rightarrow\!\!\!\!\!\to 1
and
\mathsf{d}(A):1\lhook\joinrel\rightarrow A=A
.

Then for a pair
\mathbf{k}:A\lhook\joinrel\rightarrow B\rightarrow\!\!\!\!\!\to C
and
\mathbf{l}:P\lhook\joinrel\rightarrow Q\rightarrow\!\!\!\!\!\to R
of two exact sequences one can also define the following
split sequence of
\mathbf{k}
and
\mathbf{l}
:

\displaystyle\mathsf{s}(\mathbf{k})\times\mathsf{d}(\mathbf{l}):\mathbf{k}\lhook\joinrel\rightarrow\mathbf{k}\times\mathbf{l}\rightarrow\!\!\!\!\!\to\mathbf{l}

\textstyle{\mathbf{k}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\hskip-34.14322pt:}
\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{C\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathbf{k}\times\mathbf{l}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\hskip-34.14322pt:}
\textstyle{A\times P\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{B\times Q\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{C\times R\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathbf{l}}
\textstyle{\hskip-34.14322pt:}
\textstyle{P\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{Q\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{R}

Also for each short exact sequence
\mathbf{u}:A\lhook\joinrel\rightarrow B\rightarrow\!\!\!\!\!\to C
one can define the following exact
(3\times 3)
-diagram

in which
K,A,L
are normal subgroups of
B
and

(18)

\displaystyle\ \ A

\displaystyle=\ker(\rho)=\eta^{-1}(m{\mathbb{Z}}),

\displaystyle\ \ L

\displaystyle=\ker(\eta),

\displaystyle\ \ K

\displaystyle=A\cap L.

Fix an element
g\in B
and let
L_{0}\subset L
be a subgroup.
Denote
K_{0}=K\cap L_{0}
and
M_{0}=L_{0}/K_{0}
, so we get a short exact sequence
\mathbf{u}:K_{0}\lhook\joinrel\rightarrow L_{0}\rightarrow\!\!\!\!\!\to M_{0}
.

Also let
L_{i}:=g^{-i}L_{0}g^{i}
and
K_{i}:=g^{-i}K_{0}g^{i}
for
i=0,\ldots,m-1
.
Then
L_{i}\subset L
and
K_{i}\subset K
since
L
and
K
are normal.

Lemma 2.9.1
.

[
44
, Lemma 2.3]
, cf.
[
30
]
.

Suppose that

(a)

\eta(g)=1
and
g^{m}
commutes with
L
;

(b)

L
splits into the product subgroups
L_{0},\ldots,L_{m-1}
, i.e. those subgroups generate
L
, pairwise commute, and
L_{i}\cap L_{j}=\{e\}
for all
i\not=j
;

(c)

K_{0},\ldots,K_{m-1}
generate
K
.

Then the map
\beta:L_{0}\mathop{\wr}\limits_{m}{\mathbb{Z}}\to B
defined by

\beta(b_{0},b_{1},\ldots,b_{m-1},k)=\\
=b_{0}\,(g^{-1}b_{1}g^{1})\,(g^{-2}b_{2}g^{2})\,\cdots\,(g^{-m+1}b_{m-1}g^{m-1})g^{k}=\\
=b_{0}\,g^{-1}\,b_{1}\,\cdots\,g^{-1}\,b_{{}m-1}\,g^{-1+m+k},

for
b_{i}\in L_{0}
,
i=0,\ldots,m-1
, and
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}
, is an isomorphism of groups inducing an isomorphism of exact
(3\times 3)
-diagrams:

\textstyle{K_{0}^{m}\times 0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{L_{0}^{m}\times 0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{M_{0}^{m}\times 0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\ K\ \ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\ L\ \ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\ M\ \ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{K_{0}^{m}\times m{\mathbb{Z}}\ \ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\alpha^{\prime}}
\textstyle{L_{0}\mathop{\wr}\limits_{m}{\mathbb{Z}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\beta^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{p}
\textstyle{M_{0}\wr{\mathbb{Z}}_{m}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\penalty\ \penalty\ \beta\penalty\ \penalty\ }
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{\ A\ \ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\ B\ \ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\rho}
\scriptstyle{\eta}
\textstyle{\ C\ \ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{m{\mathbb{Z}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\mathbb{Z}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\mathbb{Z}}_{m}}
\scriptstyle{\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathrm{id}\penalty\ \penalty\ }
\textstyle{m{\mathbb{Z}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\mathbb{Z}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\mathbb{Z}}_{m}}

being identity on the lower sequence.
In other words, we get an
‘‘isomorphism over
\mathbf{z}_{m}
’’
of short exact sequences

\mathbf{u}^{m}\lhook\joinrel\rightarrow\mathbf{u}\wr\mathbf{z}_{m}\rightarrow\!\!\!\!\!\to\mathbf{z}_{m}
and
\mathbf{k}\lhook\joinrel\rightarrow\mathbf{l}\rightarrow\!\!\!\!\!\to\mathbf{z}_{m}
.

in which

\displaystyle A

\displaystyle=\ker(\rho)=\eta^{-1}(m{\mathbb{Z}}\times n{\mathbb{Z}}),

\displaystyle=\ker(\eta),

\displaystyle K

\displaystyle=A\cap L.

Let
L_{0}\subset L
be a subgroup,
K_{0}=K\cap L_{0}
and
M_{0}=L_{0}/K_{0}
, so we get a short exact sequence
\mathbf{u}:K_{0}\lhook\joinrel\rightarrow L_{0}\rightarrow\!\!\!\!\!\to M_{0}
.
Fix also two elements

,

\displaystyle L_{i,j}

\displaystyle:=g^{i}\,h^{j}\,L_{0}\,h^{-j}\,g^{-i}.

Then
K_{i,j}\subset K
and
L_{i,j}\subset L
since
K
and
L
are normal.
The following lemma can be proved similarly to Lemma

.

Lemma 2.10.1
.

Suppose that

gh=hg
,
\eta(g)=(1,0)
,

, and both

and

, i.e. those subgroups generate
L
, pairwise commute, and
L_{i,j}\cap L_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime}}=\{e\}
for all

;

\{K_{i,j}\}

.

Then the map
\beta:L_{0}\mathop{\wr}\limits_{m,n}{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}\to B

In other words, we get an

3.
Homogeneous polynomials without multiple factors

The fundamental theorem of algebra implies that every real homogeneous polynomial

g:{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\to{\mathbb{R}}
is a product of finitely many linear
L_{i}=a_{i}x+b_{i}y
and irreducible over
{\mathbb{R}}
quadratic factors
Q_{j}(x,y)=c_{j}x^{2}+2d_{j}xy+e_{j}y^{2}
:

g(x,y)=\prod_{i=1}^{p}L_{i}(x,y)\cdot\prod_{j=1}^{q}Q_{j}(x,y).

Evidently,
g
has critical points if and only if
\deg g\geq 2
.
Moreover, if
g
has two proportional linear factors, that is
L_{i}=sL_{j}
for some
i\not=j
and
s\not=0
, then all the line
L_{i}=0
consists of critical points of
g
.
This implies that
the origin
0\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
is a unique critical point of
g
iff
\deg g\geq 2
and
g
has no multiple linear factors
.

In the latter case the number
p
of linear factors can be seen from the topological structure of level sets of
g
, see Figure
1
.

p=0

p=1

p=2

p=3

Figure 1.
Topological structure of level-sets of homogeneous polynomials without multiple factors

We will say that
0\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
is

\bullet

a
non-degenerate extreme
if
p=0
and
q=1
, so
g=Q_{1}
;

\bullet

a
degenerate extreme
if
p=0
and
q\geq 2
, so
g=Q_{1}Q_{2}\cdots Q_{q}
;

\bullet

a
quasi saddle
if
p=1
, so
g=L_{1}\cdot Q_{1}\cdots Q_{q}
;

\bullet

a
non-degenerate saddle
if
p=2
and
q=0
, so
g=L_{1}L_{2}
;

\bullet

a
saddle
if
p\geq 2
and
\deg g=p+2q\geq 3
.

3.1.
Symmetries

Let
g:{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\to{\mathbb{R}}
be a homogeneous polynomial without multiple factors.
Let also
\mathcal{S}(g)
be the group of
germs
at
0\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
of
diffeomorphisms

h:({\mathbb{R}}^{2},0)\to({\mathbb{R}}^{2},0)
such that
g\circ h=g
, and
\mathcal{L}(g)\subset\mathrm{GL}({\mathbb{R}},2)
be the group of
linear isomorphisms

A:{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
which also preserve
g
, that is
g(Ax)=g(x)
for all
x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
.
Then
\mathcal{L}(g)
can be regarded as a subgroup of
\mathcal{S}(g)
.

Let
h\in\mathcal{S}(g)
, and
A=J(h)
be its Jacobi matrix at
0
.
Since
g
is homogeneous, say of degree
k
, the identity
g\circ h=g
easily implies that
g(Ax)=g(x)
for all
x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
,
[
32
, Lemma 36]
:

g(x)=\frac{g(tx)}{t^{k}}=\frac{g\bigl(h(tx)\bigr)}{t^{k}}=g\left(\frac{h(tx)}{t}\right)\xrightarrow[t\to 0]{}g(Ax).

In other words,
if a diffeomorphism preserves a homogeneous polynomial
g
, then its Jacobi matrix at
0
also preserves
g
.
Hence we get a natural homomorphism:

J:\mathcal{S}(g)\to\mathcal{L}(g),\qquad h\mapsto J(h).

Since for a linear map given by a matrix
A
its Jacobi matrix is
A
, we see that
J
is the identity on
\mathcal{L}(g)
.
In other words,
J
is a
retraction
of
\mathcal{S}(g)
onto
\mathcal{L}(g)
, and in particular it is
surjective
.

Let also
\mathcal{L}^{+}(g)=\mathcal{L}(g)\cap\mathrm{GL}^{+}({\mathbb{R}},2)
and
\mathcal{S}^{+}(g)
be the subgroup of
\mathcal{S}(g)
consisting of orientation preserving germs.
Then

J(\mathcal{S}^{+}(g))=\mathcal{L}^{+}(g).

Lemma 3.1.1
.

[
37
, Lemma 6.2]
.

After a proper linear change of coordinates in
{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
and replacing (if necessary)
g
with
-g
, one can assume that the following properties hold.

\mathrm{(a)}

Suppose
0\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
is a
non-degenerate local extreme
, i.e.
\deg g=2
, and
g
is an irreducible quadratic form.
Then

\displaystyle g(x,y)

\displaystyle=x^{2}+y^{2},

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}(g)

\displaystyle=O(2),

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}^{+}(g)

\displaystyle=SO(2).

\mathrm{(b)}

If
0\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
is a
non-degenerate saddle
, so
\deg g=2
, and
g
is a product of two independent linear factors, then
g(x,y)=xy
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}(g)

\displaystyle=\bigl\{\pm\left(\begin{smallmatrix}t^{-1}&0\\
0&t\end{smallmatrix}\right)\mid t\not=0\bigr\},

\displaystyle\ \mathcal{L}^{+}(g)

\displaystyle=\bigl\{\left(\begin{smallmatrix}t^{-1}&0\\
0&t\end{smallmatrix}\right)\mid t\not=0\bigr\}.

\mathrm{(c)}

Finally, assume that
0\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
is a
degenerate
critical point of
g
, so
\deg g\geq 3
.
Then

\mathrm{(a)}

\mathcal{L}^{+}(g)
is a finite cyclic subgroup of
SO(2)
of some order
m\geq 1
generated by the rotation by
\frac{2\pi}{m}
;

\mathrm{(b)}

\mathcal{L}(g)
either coincides with
\mathcal{L}^{+}(g)
or it is a dihedral group
\mathbb{D}_{m}
of order
2m
generated by
\mathcal{L}^{+}(g)
and the reflection
(x,y)\mapsto(-x,y)
;

\mathrm{(c)}

if
\deg g
is even, e.g.
z
is a
degenerate local extreme
, then
m
is always even, since
\mathcal{L}^{+}(g)
contains the map
q(x,y)=(-x,-y)
.

Remark 3.1.2
.

Statements

(
a
)

and

(
c
)

of Lemma
3.1.1
indicate an essential difference between diffeomorphisms preserving degenerate and non-degenerate local extremes.
Suppose we have a continuous family

h_{t}=(p_{t},q_{t}):({\mathbb{R}}^{2},0)\to({\mathbb{R}}^{2},0),\quad t\in[0,1],

of germs of diffeomorphisms preserving a homogeneous polynomial
g(x,y)
, such that this family defines a continuous path in
\mathcal{S}^{+}(g)
‘‘with respect to at least
\mathcal{C}^{1}
topology’’, which mean that the Jacobi matrices
J(h_{t})
are continuous in
t
.

If, for example,
g(x,y)=x^{2}+y^{2}
, so
p_{t}(x,y)^{2}+q_{t}(x,y)^{2}\equiv x^{2}+y^{2}
, then
the matrices
J(h_{t})
can be arbitrary rotations
.
On the other hand, if
g(x,y)=x^{4}+y^{4}
, i.e.
p_{t}(x,y)^{4}+q_{t}(x,y)^{4}\equiv x^{4}+y^{4}
, then there are only finitely many possibilities for
J(h_{t})
.
Actually, in this case
J(h_{t})
can only be a rotation by
\frac{k\pi}{2}
for
k=0,1,2,3
.
Therefore continuity of
J(h_{t})
in
t
implies that now
J(h_{t})
must be the same for all
t\in[0,1]
.

3.2.
Symmetry index of a degenerate local extreme

Suppose that
0\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
is a degenerate local extreme of
g
, as in the in the case 3

(
c
)

(
c
)

of Lemma
3.1.1
.
The order
m
of the cyclic group
\mathcal{L}(g)
will be called the
symmetry index
of
0\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
.

Example 3.2.1
.

Let

g(x,y)=x^{4}+y^{4}=(x^{2}+\sqrt{2}xy+y^{2})(x^{2}-\sqrt{2}xy+y^{2}).

Then
\mathcal{L}^{+}(g)\cong{\mathbb{Z}}_{4}
is generated by rotation by
\pi/2
:
r(x,y)=(-y,x)
, and
\mathcal{L}(g)
is isomorphic to the dihedral group
\mathbb{D}_{4}
generated by
r
and the reflection
s(x,y)=(-x,y)
.
Thus here
m=4
.

3.3.
Framings at a degenerate local extreme

Let
v\in T_{0}{\mathbb{R}}^{2}={\mathbb{R}}^{2}
be a non-zero tangent vector at
0\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
.
Then its orbit

\Phi(0,v)=\{T_{0}h(v)\mid h\in\mathcal{S}(g)\}

with respect to
\mathcal{L}(g)
, and thus with respect to
\mathcal{S}(g)
, consists of either
2m
or
m
vectors that are cyclically order, see Figure
3
.

This set
\Phi(0,v)
will be called a
framing
at
0\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
, whenever either of the following equivalent conditions hold:

(a)

the action of
\mathcal{L}(g)
to
\Phi(0,v)
is
effective
, i.e. there is a non-unit element
A\in\mathcal{L}(g)
fixed on
\Phi(0,v)
;

(b)

if
h\in\mathcal{S}(g)
is such that
T_{0}h(w)=w
for all
w\in\Phi(0,v)
, then
T_{0}h=\mathrm{id}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}
.

Roughly speaking the elements of
\mathcal{L}(g)
can be distinguished by their action on the finite set
\Phi(0,v)
.

Example 3.3.1
.

Let
g(x,y)=(x^{2}+y^{2})(x^{2}+2y^{2})
.
Then
\mathcal{L}^{+}(g)\cong{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
is generated by
-\mathrm{id}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}
, while
\mathcal{L}(g)
is the dihedral group
\mathbb{D}_{2}\cong{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}\times{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
generated by
-\mathrm{id}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}
and the reflection
s(x,y)=(-x,y)
.
Here
m=2
.
Also, if
v=(0,1)\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
, then
\Phi(0,v)=\{\pm v\}
is not a framing, since
s
trivially acts on
\Phi(0,v)
.
For any other vector
w\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
which is not collinear to
v
, its orbit
\Phi(0,w)
is a framing.

Example 3.3.2
.

Let
g(x,y)=(x^{2}+y^{2})(3x^{2}+2y^{2})(x^{2}+xy+y^{2})
.
Then
\mathcal{L}(g)=\mathcal{L}^{+}(g)\cong{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
is generated by
-\mathrm{id}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}
.
Here
m=2
as well and for any non-zero vector
w\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
its orbit
\Phi(0,w)
is a framing.

The following easy lemma shows that one can always choose
v
so that
\Phi(0,v)
is a framing.

Lemma 3.3.3
.

The set
\Phi(0,v)
is
not a framing
iff the following two conditions hold:

(a)

\mathcal{L}(g)
is the dihedral group
\mathbb{D}_{2}\cong{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}\times{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
, so
m=2
;

(b)

v
is fixed under the reflection from
\mathcal{L}(g)
.

4.
Space of maps
\mathcal{F}(M,P)

In this section we will describe several properties of maps belonging to
\mathcal{F}(M,P)
.
Recall that by definition a map
f:M\to P
belongs to
\mathcal{F}(M,P)
if it satisfies axioms
(B)
and
(L)
.

In the case when
P=S^{1}
one can also say about local extremes of
f
, and even about local minimums or maximums if we fix an orientation of
S^{1}
.
Moreover, since
S^{1}
is a group
{\mathbb{R}}/{\mathbb{Z}}
, one can say about neighborhoods of a point
c\in S^{1}
of the form
(c-\varepsilon;c+\varepsilon)
for small
\varepsilon>0
.

First we will study structure of maps from
\mathcal{F}(M,P)
at their critical points.

4.1.
Smooth functions on the plane with isolated critical points

Let
g:{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\equiv{\mathbb{C}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
be a
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}
function such that
0\in{\mathbb{C}}
is an isolated critical point and
g(0)=0
.
Then there are germs of homeomorphisms
h:({\mathbb{C}},0)\to({\mathbb{C}},0)
and
\phi:({\mathbb{R}},0)\to({\mathbb{R}},0)
such that

(21)

\phi\circ g\circ h(z)=\begin{cases}|z|^{2},&\text{if $0\in{\mathbb{C}}$ is a local extreme\/ of $f$, \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{Dancer:2:JRAM:1987}{}{}]}},\\
Re(z^{m}),&\text{otherwise, \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{ChurchTimourian:PJM:1973, Prishlyak:TA:2002}{}{}]}}.\end{cases}

In particular, critical points of homogeneous polynomials without multiple factors cover all possible topological types of isolated critical points of maps
{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\to{\mathbb{R}}
.

4.2.
f
-adapted subsurfaces

Now let
f\in{\mathcal{F}}(M,P)
.
As mentioned above condition
(L)
implies that each critical point of
z\in\Sigma_{f}
of
f
is isolated.

A connected component
K
of a level-set
f^{-1}(c)
,
c\in P
, will be called a
leaf
(of
f
).
We also call
K

regular
if it contains no critical points, and
critical
otherwise.

Evidently, a regular leaf of
f
is a submanifold of
M
diffeomorphic to the circle.
On the other hand, it follows from Axiom
(L)
, (see also Figure
1
) that a critical leaf
K
has a structure of a
1
-dimensional CW-complex whose
0
-cells are critical points of
f
belonging to
K
.
Notice that if
K
contains only quasi-saddles of
f
, see Figure
1
b), then it is a smooth submanifold of
M
diffeomorphic to the circle, however it is still
critical
as a leaf of
f
.

Let
K
be a (regular or critical) leaf of
f
.
For
\varepsilon>0
let
N_{\varepsilon}
be the connected component of
f^{-1}[c-\varepsilon,c+\varepsilon]
containing
K
.
Then
N_{\varepsilon}
will be called an
f
-regular neighborhood of
K
if
\varepsilon
is so small that
N_{\varepsilon}\setminus K
contains no critical points of
f
and no boundary components of
\partial M
.

A submanifold
V\subset M
will be called
f
-adapted
if
V=\mathop{\cup}\limits_{i=1}^{a}A_{i}
, where each
A_{i}
is either a critical point of
f
or a regular leaf of
f
or an
f
-regular neighborhood of some (regular or critical) leaf of
f
.
We will denote by
V^{(i)}
,
i=0,1,2
, the union of connected components of
V
of dimension
i
.

Notice that if
V
is an
f
-adapted subsurface, then
f|_{V}:V\to P
satisfies Axioms
(B)
and
(L)
, that is
f|_{V}\in{\mathcal{F}}(V,P)
.

4.3.
Graph of
f\in\mathcal{F}(M,P)

Consider the partition
\Gamma_{f}
of
M
into the leaves of
f
, and let
p:M\to\Gamma_{f}
be the natural map associating to each
x\in M
the corresponding element of
\Gamma_{f}
containing
x
.
Endow
\Gamma_{f}
with the quotient topology, so a subset (a collection of leaves)
A\subset\Gamma_{f}
is open iff
p^{-1}(A)
(that is their union) is open in
M
.
It follows from axioms
(B)
and
(L)
that
\Gamma_{f}
has a natural structure of
1
-dimensional CW-complex, whose
0
-cells correspond to boundary components of
M
and critical leaves of
f
.
We will call
\Gamma_{f}
the
graph
of
f
.

Since by definition
f
takes constant values on elements of
\Gamma_{f}
, it induces a function
\widehat{f}:\Gamma_{f}\to P
such that
f=\widehat{f}\circ p
, see Figure
2
.

Figure 2.

Remark 4.3.1
.

This graph was independently introduced in the papers by G. Adelson-Welsky and A. Kronrod
[
1
]
, and G. Reeb
[
50
]
, and often called
Kronrod-Reeb
or
Reeb
graph of
f
.
It is a useful tool for understanding the topological structure of smooth functions on surfaces, e.g.
[
22
]
,
[
2
]
.
It plays as well an important role in a theory of dynamical systems on manifolds and called
Lyapunov graph
of
f
following J. Frank
[
13
,
5
,
54
,
6
]
.
The reason is that for generic Morse maps
\Gamma_{f}
can be embedded into
M
, so that
f
will be monotone on its edges.

Remark 4.3.2
.

Notice that if
M
is a torus or a Klein bottle, then there exists a locally trivial fibrations
f:M\to S^{1}
, see maps
(D)
and
(E)
in Theorem
5.2.2
.
Such a map has no critical points and so
f\in\mathcal{F}(M,S^{1})
.
Evidently, the graph
\Gamma_{f}
of
f
is a circle and it has no ‘‘vertices’’ that correspond to critical leaves.
In this case we assume that
\Gamma_{f}
consists of one edge (homeomorphic to the circle) and has no vertices.

Lemma 4.3.3
.

The induced maps
p_{1}:H_{1}(M,{\mathbb{Z}})\to H_{1}(\Gamma_{f},{\mathbb{Z}})
of homology groups is surjective and if
H_{1}(M,{\mathbb{Z}})\not=0
, always have a non-trivial kernel.
Therefore if
M=S^{2}
,
D^{2}
,
S^{1}\times[0,1]
,
{\mathbb{R}}{P}^{2}
, Möbius band, then
\Gamma_{f}
is a tree.
If
M=T^{2}
or Klein bottle, then
\Gamma_{f}
is either a tree or has a unique cycle.

Denote by
\mathcal{H}(\Gamma_{f})
the group of homeomorphisms of
\Gamma_{f}
.
Then for each
h\in\mathcal{S}(f)
the identity
f\circ h=f
implies that
h(f^{-1}(c))=f^{-1}(c)
for all
c\in P
.
Thus
h
leaves invariant every level set of
f
, and in particular induces a certain permutation
\rho(h)
of connected components of
f^{-1}(c)
, i.e. leaves of
f
being in turn points of
\Gamma_{f}
.
On other words, we get a map
\rho(h):\Gamma_{f}\to\Gamma_{f}
.
One can easily check that
\rho(h)
is a
homeomorphism
of
\Gamma_{f}
making commutative the following diagram:

(22)

\displaystyle\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{P}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces

Moreover, the correspondence
h\mapsto\rho(h)
is a
homomorphism
of groups

(23)

\rho:\mathcal{S}(f)\to\mathcal{H}(\Gamma_{f}).

4.4.
Enhanced graph of
f\in{\mathcal{F}}(M,P)

In order to encode an information coming from
degenerate local extremes
of
f
, see Remark
3.1.2
, we will add to
\Gamma_{f}
new edges corresponding to framings at such points.

Let
z
be a
degenerate
local extreme of
f
and
v\in T_{z}M
, and

\Phi(z,v)=\{T_{z}h(v)\mid h\in\mathcal{S}(f,z)\}\ \subset\ T_{z}M

be its orbit with respect to the action of
\mathcal{S}(f,z)
.
Similarly, to §
3.3
, we will say that
\Phi(z,v)
is a
framing at
z
if for every
h\in\mathcal{S}(f,z)
such that
h
fixes each element from
\Phi(z,v)
the tangent map
T_{z}h:T_{z}M\to T_{z}M
is the identity.

Let
z_{i}
,
(i=1,\ldots,l)
, be all the
degenerate
local extremes of
f
, and
\Phi_{i}=\Phi(z_{i},v^{i}_{0})
be some framing at
z_{i}
containing
k_{i}
edges.
We will say that such a collection of framings
\Phi=\{\Phi_{i}\}_{i=1,\ldots,l}
is
f
-adapted
, if it is invariant with respect to
\mathcal{S}(f)
, that is if
h\in\mathcal{S}(f)
and
h(z_{i})=z_{j}
for some
i,j
, then
T_{z_{i}}h(\Phi_{i})=\Phi_{j}
.
One easily checks, that
f
-adapted framings always exist,
[
40
, Corolary 1]
.

a) Framing at a degenerate local

b) Enhanced graph

extreme with symmetry index
m=6

Figure 3.

Thus
\mathcal{S}(f)
naturally acts on
\Gamma_{f}
as well as on each
f
-adapted framing
\Phi
via the corresponding differentials of maps.
We want to ‘‘join’’ these actions.

Regard every point
z_{i}
,
i=1,\ldots,l
, as a vertex of
\Gamma_{f}
of degree
1
, and glue to this vertex
k_{i}
edges
e^{i}_{0},\ldots,e^{i}_{k_{i}-1}
corresponding to the vectors in the corresponding framing
\Phi(z_{i},v^{i}_{0})
, see Figure
3
b).
The obtained graph will be denoted by
\widehat{\Gamma}_{f}
and called the
enhanced
graph of
f
.

One can assume that each new edge has length
1
.
Then the action of
\mathcal{S}(f)
on
\Gamma_{f}
extends to a unique action of
\mathcal{S}(f)
on
\widehat{\Gamma}_{f}
so that each
h\in\mathcal{S}(f)
interchanges edges (via length preserving maps) of
\widehat{\Gamma}_{f}\setminus\Gamma_{f}
in the same way as its tangent map
Th
interchanges vectors in the
f
-adapted framing
\Phi
.

4.5.
Action of
\mathcal{S}(f)
on
\widehat{\Gamma}_{f}

The previous paragraphs means that the homomorphism (
23
) extends to a homomorphism

(24)

\rho:\mathcal{S}(f)\to\mathcal{H}(\widehat{\Gamma}_{f}).

associating to each
h\in\mathcal{S}(f)
the induced homeomorphism of
\widehat{\Gamma}_{f}
.
Let

\displaystyle\Delta(f)

\displaystyle=\ker(\rho),

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}(f)

\displaystyle=\rho(\mathcal{S}(f)),

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}(f,V)

\displaystyle=\rho(\mathcal{S}(f,V)),

where
V
is any
f
-adapted submanifold.
Then, we obtain the following short exact sequence:

(25)

\mathbf{b}(f,V):\quad\Delta(f,V)\lhook\joinrel\rightarrow\mathcal{S}(f,V)\rightarrow\!\!\!\!\!\to\mathcal{G}(f,V).

Evidently,
h\in\mathcal{S}(f)
belongs to
\Delta(f)
if and only if

•

h
preserves every leaf of
f
;

•

and for every
degenerate local extreme

z
(being also a critical leaf) of
f
the corresponding tangent map
T_{z}h:T_{z}M\to T_{z}M
of
h
at
z
is the identity.

For an
f
-adapted submanifold
V
let

(26)

\displaystyle\Delta^{\prime}(f,V)

\displaystyle=\Delta(f)\cap\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{id}}(M,V),

(27)

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(f,V)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{S}(f)\cap\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{id}}(M,V),

(28)

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}^{\prime}(f,V)

\displaystyle=\rho(\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(f,V)).

Lemma 4.5.1
.

e.g.
[
21
, Lemma 2.2]

The group
\mathcal{G}(f,V)
is finite.
Moreover, if
f
is a generic Morse map, then
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}(f,V)
is trivial.

Lemma 4.5.2

(
[
44
, Lemma 4.1]
)
.

\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{id}}(f,V)
is the identity path component of
\Delta^{\prime}(f,V)
.
Therefore
\Delta^{\prime}(f,V)
is a disjoint union of some path components of
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(f,V)
, and the inclusion
\Delta^{\prime}(f,V)\subset\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(f,V)
induces a monomorphism

\pi_{0}\Delta^{\prime}(f,V)\lhook\joinrel\rightarrow\pi_{0}\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(f,V),

whence

(29)

\mathcal{G}^{\prime}(f,V)\cong\frac{\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(f,V)}{\Delta^{\prime}(f,V)}\cong\frac{\pi_{0}\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(f,V)}{\pi_{0}\Delta^{\prime}(f,V)}.

It follows from (
29
) that we have another short exact sequence

(30)

\mathbf{b}^{\prime}(f,V):\quad\pi_{0}\Delta^{\prime}(f,V)\lhook\joinrel\rightarrow\pi_{0}\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(f,V)\rightarrow\!\!\!\!\!\to\mathcal{G}^{\prime}(f,V).

This sequence will be called the
Biebarbach sequence of the pair
(f,V)
.
It plays the main role in our considerations.

4.6.
Simplification results

The following Lemmas
4.6.1
and
4.6.2
reduce computation of
\mathbf{b}^{\prime}(f,V)
to the case when
M
is connected and
V
consists of critical points of
f
and boundary components of
M
.
Let
R_{V}
be a
f
-regular neighborhood of
V
.

Lemma 4.6.1
.

(
[
44
, Corollary 7.2]
)

The natural inclusions of pairs

(31)

\displaystyle\bigl(\mathcal{S}(f,R_{V}),\Delta(f,R_{V})\bigr)\ \subset\ \bigl(\mathcal{S}(f,V),\Delta(f,V)\bigr),

(32)

\displaystyle\bigl(\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(f,R_{V}),\Delta^{\prime}(f,R_{V})\bigr)\ \subset\ \bigl(\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(f,V),\Delta^{\prime}(f,V)\bigr),

are homotopy equivalences, and therefore they induce isomorphisms of the corresponding sequences:

\displaystyle\mathbf{b}(f,R_{V})

\displaystyle\cong\mathbf{b}(f,V),

\displaystyle\mathbf{b}^{\prime}(f,R_{V})

\displaystyle\cong\mathbf{b}^{\prime}(f,V).

Lemma 4.6.2

(cf.
[
44
, Lemma 5.2]
)
.

Let
X_{1},\ldots,X_{k}
all the connected components of
\overline{M\setminus R_{V}}
, and
{X}^{\partial}_{i}:=X_{i}\cap R_{V}
,
i=1,\ldots,k
, so
{X}^{\partial}_{i}
consists of some common boundary components of
X_{i}
and
R_{V}
.
Then the natural homomorphism

\alpha:\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{id}}(M,V)\to\prod_{i=1}^{k}\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{id}}(X_{i},{X}^{\partial}_{i}),\qquad\alpha(h)=\bigl(h|_{X_{1}},\ldots,h|_{X_{k}}\bigr),

induces an isomorphism
\mathbf{b}^{\prime}(f,V)\cong\prod\limits_{i=1}^{k}\mathbf{b}^{\prime}(f|_{X_{i}},{X}^{\partial}_{i})
.

If
M
contains no connected components
M_{1}
and
M_{2}
that are diffeomorphic each other and do not intersect
V
, then we also have an isomorphism
\mathbf{b}(f,V)\cong\prod\limits_{i=1}^{k}\mathbf{b}(f|_{X_{i}},{X}^{\partial}_{i})
.

Notes to the proof.

In fact, Lemma 5.2 in
[
44
]

wrongly
states that we always have an isomorphism of sequences
\mathbf{b}(f,V)\cong\prod_{i=1}^{k}\mathbf{b}(f|_{X_{i}},{X}^{\partial}_{i})
.
The gap is in the claim that the map
\alpha:\mathcal{D}(M,U)\to\prod_{i=1}^{k}\mathcal{D}(X_{i},U_{i})
given by the same formula
\alpha(h)=\bigl(h|_{X_{1}},\ldots,h|_{X_{k}}\bigr)
is
well-defined
, where
U_{i}
is a
f
-regular neighborhood of
{X}^{\partial}_{i}
in
X_{i}
, and
U=\cup_{i=1}^{k}U_{i}
.

Indeed, the definition of
\alpha
assumes that every
h\in\mathcal{D}(M,U)
leaves invariant each connected component of
M
.
However, it might happen that
M
has two diffeomorphic connected components
M_{1}
and
M_{2}
which do not intersect
U
.
Then
\alpha
is not-defined for a diffeomorphism of
M
interchanging
M_{1}
and
M_{2}
and fixed on
M\setminus(M_{1}\cup M_{2})
.

Nevertheless, the statement about isomorphism of
{\mathbf{b}^{\prime}}\!
-sequences remains true, since every isotopic to the identity diffeomorphism preserves every connected component.
∎

The following lemma allows to cut an
f
-adapted collar of some boundary component of
M
.

Lemma 4.6.3
.

(
[
44
, Theorem 5.5(3)]
)

Let
M
be a not necessarily orientable connected compact surface,
f\in{\mathcal{F}}(M,P)
, and
V
be a connected component of
\partial M
.
Suppose there exists a regular component
W
of some level set of
f
separating
M
and let
B
and
A
be the connected components of
\overline{M\setminus W}
.
Assume that
V\subset A
and let
X\subset B\setminus W
be an
f
-adapted submanifold, see Figure
4
.

Figure 4
.

Suppose
A
is an
annulus
and
h(A)=A
for all
h\in\mathcal{S}(f,X\cup V)
.
Then we have the following homotopy equivalence:

\mathcal{S}(f,X\cup V)\cong\mathcal{S}(f|_{B},X\cup W)\times\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(f|_{A},\partial A)

which induces the homotopy equivalence

\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(f,X\cup V)\cong\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(f|_{B},X\cup W)\times\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(f|_{A},\partial A)

and homotopy equivalences between the corresponding
\Delta
- and
\Delta^{\prime}
-groups.
In particular, we get isomorphisms of short exact sequences:

(33)

\displaystyle\mathbf{b}(f,X\cup V)

\displaystyle\cong\mathbf{b}(f|_{B},X\cup W)\times\mathbf{b}^{\prime}(f|_{A},\partial A),

\displaystyle\mathbf{b}^{\prime}(f,X\cup V)

\displaystyle\cong\mathbf{b}^{\prime}(f|_{B},X\cup W)\times\mathbf{b}^{\prime}(f|_{A},\partial A).

Moreover, if
B
is either a
2
-disk or an annulus and
X=\varnothing
, then

(34)

\mathbf{b}^{\prime}(f,\partial M)\cong\mathbf{b}^{\prime}(f|_{B},\partial B)\times\mathbf{b}^{\prime}(f|_{A},\partial A).

4.7.
Structure of
\pi_{0}\Delta(f,V)

Fix a possibly empty collection
V
of boundary components of
M
.

Let
e
be an (open) edge of
\Gamma_{f}
, and
x\in e
be a point, so it corresponds to a regular leaf
\gamma
of
f
in
\mathrm{Int}{M}
.
Then there exists a Dehn twist
\tau_{\gamma}\in\Delta(f)
along
\gamma
supported in
f
-regular neighborhood of
\gamma
which does not intersect
\partial M
.
We will call a
Dehn twist around the edge
e
and also denote by
\tau_{e}
.

Notice that by definition
\tau_{e}
is fixed on
\partial M\supset V
.
Then its isotopy class in
\pi_{0}\Delta(f,V)
will be denoted by
[\tau_{e}]
.
Since for distinct edges
e,e^{\prime}
their Dehn twists
\tau_{e}
and
\tau_{e^{\prime}}
have disjoint supports, we obtain that
\tau_{e}
and
\tau_{e^{\prime}}
commute.
Hence their isotopy classes
[\tau_{e}]
and
[\tau_{e^{\prime}}]
in
\pi_{0}\Delta(f,V)
also commute.

Let also
v_{0},v_{1}
be the vertices of
e
, and
K_{i}=p^{-1}(v_{i})
,
i=0,1
, be the corresponding leaves of
f
.
Then each
K_{i}
is either a critical leaf of
f
or a boundary components of
M
.
We will say that
e
(as well as the corresponding Dehn twist
\tau_{e}
is
internal for
V
if each
K_{i}
is one of following leaves:

•

either a degenerate local extreme,

•

or contains a saddle point of
f
,

•

or is contained in
V
.

Otherwise
e
is
external with respect to
V
.

Lemma 4.7.1
.

(e.g.
[
34
, Theorem 6.2]
)

Let
V
be a possibly empty collection of boundary components of
M
.

(1)

An edge
e
of
\Gamma_{f}
is
external
, if and only if
\tau_{e}\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{id}}(f)
.

(2)

Suppose
K
is a Klein bottle, and
f:K\to S^{1}
is a map without critical points, see map
(E)
in Theorem
5.2.2
.
Then there exists an isotopy
H:K\times[0,1]\to K
such that
H_{0}=\mathrm{id}_{K}
and
H_{1}\in\Delta^{\prime}(f)
preserves each leaf of
f
and reverses its orientation.
In this case
\pi_{0}\Delta^{\prime}(f,V)\cong 0
and
\pi_{0}\Delta(f,V)\cong{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
.

(3)

In all other cases
\pi_{0}\Delta(f,V)
is a free abelian group freely generated by internal for
V
Dehn twists, so
\pi_{0}\Delta(f,V)\cong{\mathbb{Z}}^{k}
.
Hence
\pi_{0}\Delta^{\prime}(f,V)
is also a free abelian group generated by
relations between internal Dehn twists in
\pi_{0}\mathcal{D}(M,V)
.

5.
Homotopy types of stabilizers and orbits

In this section we assume that
M
is a compact surface,
f\in\mathcal{F}(M,P)
, and
V
is
f
-adapted submanifold.
We will expose the results about homotopy types of
\mathcal{S}(f,V)
and orbits of
\mathcal{O}(f,V)
.
Everywhere the sign
\simeq
means ‘‘
homotopy equivalent
’’.
For a set
V
we will denote by
|V|
the number of points in
V
if it is finite, and
\infty
otherwise.

Theorem 5.1

(
[
51
,
34
]
)
.

(1)

The natural map into the orbit

\mathsf{p}:\mathcal{D}(M,V)\to\mathcal{O}(f,V),\qquad\mathsf{p}(h)=f\circ h

is a locally trivial principal
\mathcal{S}(f,V)
-fibration.

(2)

The restriction
\mathsf{p}:\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{id}}(M,V)\to\mathcal{O}_{f}(f,V)
is a locally trivial principal
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(f,V)
-fibration.

(3)

\mathcal{O}_{f}(f,V)=\mathcal{O}_{f}(f,V\cup X)
for any collection
X
of boundary components of
M
.

(4)

\mathcal{O}_{f}(f)
is a Frèchet manifold, whence (by
[
48
]
) it has a homotopy type of a CW-complex.

Notices to the proof.

Statements
(2)
and
(3)
are simple consequences of
(3)
(see
[
43
, Corollary 2.1]
).
Statements
(1)
and
(4)
were proved by F. Sergeraert
[
51
]
for functions
f:M\to{\mathbb{R}}
of the so-called
finite codimension
on a closed manifolds
M
and
V=\varnothing
.
Further it was extended to a more general context of tame actions of
tame Lie groups on tame Frechet spaces
in
[
34
, Appendix 11]
.
The latter includes Theorem
5.1
, see
[
44
, Theorem 1.1]
for details.
∎

Definition 5.1.1

(Weak homotopy equivalences of sequences of maps)
.

Suppose we are given two sequences of continuous maps of topological spaces:

\displaystyle\mathbf{u}:K_{1}\xrightarrow{\alpha_{1}}\cdots\xrightarrow{\alpha_{k-1}}K_{k},

\displaystyle\mathbf{v}:L_{1}\xrightarrow{\beta_{1}}\cdots\xrightarrow{\beta_{k-1}}L_{k}.

Then by a
morphism

Such a morphism will be called a
weak homotopy equivalence
, whenever each
\gamma_{i}
is a weak homotopy equivalence.
In this case we will write
\gamma:\mathbf{u}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\sf\tiny{w}}}}{{\simeq}}\mathbf{v}
.

The following sequence of maps will be called an
\mathbf{O}
-sequence of
(f,V)
:

(35)

\mathbf{O}\left(f,V\right):\quad\Delta^{\prime}(f,V)\lhook\joinrel\rightarrow{}\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(f,V)\lhook\joinrel\rightarrow{}\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{id}}(M,V)\xrightarrow{\mathsf{p}}\!\!\!\!\!\to\mathcal{O}_{f}(f,V),

where the first two arrows are natural inclusions of normal subgroups, and the last map is a projection to cosets (we denoted it by
\rightarrow\!\!\!\!\!\to
meaning that it is surjective).

We will study such sequences up to a weak homotopy equivalence and one of the main tools are long exact sequence of fibration
\mathsf{p}
:

\cdots\to\pi_{k+1}\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{id}}(f,V)\to\pi_{k+1}\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{id}}(M,V)\xrightarrow{\mathsf{p}_{k+1}}\pi_{k+1}\mathcal{O}_{f}(f,V)\xrightarrow{\partial_{k+1}}\\
\to\pi_{k}\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{id}}(f,V)\to\pi_{k}\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{id}}(M,V)\xrightarrow{\mathsf{p}_{k}}\pi_{k}\mathcal{O}_{f}(f,V)\xrightarrow{\partial_{k}}\cdots\\
\cdots\to\pi_{1}\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{id}}(M,V)\xrightarrow{\mathsf{p}_{1}}\pi_{1}\mathcal{O}_{f}(f,V)\xrightarrow{\partial_{1}}\!\!\!\!\!\to\pi_{0}\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(f,V),

where the corresponding base points are
\mathrm{id}_{M}
and
f
.

The homotopy types of groups
\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{id}}(M,V)
are computed in
[
52
, Theorem A]
,
[
7
, Theorems 1,2]
,
[
8
, Theorems 1B,1C,1D]
,
[
14
, Théorème 1]
, and are presented in the following Table
1
, in which
|V|
is the number of points in
V
,
Mo
is a Möbius band, and
K
is a Klein bottle.
Moreover, Theorem
5.2.1
below describes the homotopy type of
\mathcal{S}(f,V)
.
This will give a complete information about higher homotopy groups of spaces in (
35
) and a lot of information about fundamental groups.

Remark 5.1.2

(Explicit construction of
\partial_{1}
)
.

Let
\gamma:[0,1]\to\mathcal{O}_{f}(f,V)
be a loop with
\gamma(0)=\gamma(1)=f
, and
[\gamma]
its homotopy class in
\pi_{1}\mathcal{O}_{f}(f,V)
.
Since
\mathsf{p}
satisfies path lifting axiom, there exists a path
\widehat{\gamma}:[0,1]\to\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{id}}(M,V)
such that
\widehat{\gamma}(0)=\mathrm{id}_{M}
, and
\widehat{\gamma}(1)\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(f,V)
.
Then

\mathsf{p}_{1}([\gamma])=[\widehat{\gamma}(1)]\in\pi_{0}\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(f,V)

is the isotopy class of
\widehat{\gamma}(1)
in
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(f,V)
.

Lemma 5.1.3
.

(1)

\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{id}}(M,V)
is contractible if and only if
\chi(M)<|V|
, which in particular holds if
V
is infinite or when
\chi(M)<0
.

(2)

If
V=\varnothing
, then
\pi_{2}\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{id}}(M)=0
and for
k\geq 3
we have that

\pi_{k}\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{id}}(M)=\pi_{k}M=\begin{cases}\pi_{k}S^{3}&\text{if $M=S^{2}$ or ${\mathbb{R}}{P}^{2}$},\\
0&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

(3)

The image of
\mathsf{p}_{1}\bigl(\pi_{1}\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{id}}(M,V)\bigr)
is contained in the center of
\pi_{1}\mathcal{O}_{f}(f,V)
, (
[
43
, Lemma 2.2]
).

(4)

The set
\pi_{0}\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(f,V)
is a group, and
\partial_{1}:\pi_{1}\mathcal{O}_{f}(f,V)\to\pi_{0}\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(f,V)
is a homomorphism.

5.2.
Structure of
\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{id}}(f,V)

The following theorem is proved in
[
34
]
for Morse maps and in
[
40
]
for all
f\in\mathcal{F}(M,P)
.
In fact, it is a consequence of a series of papers
[
32
,
33
,
38
,
41
,
42
]
about diffeomorphisms preserving orbits of flows.
The technique developed in those paper is also extensively used for the proof of almost all presented here results, e.g. Lemmas
4.7.1
,
4.6.1
,
6.1.1
, Theorem
5.2.2
.

Theorem 5.2.1
.

(
[
34
,
40
]
).

Suppose
M
is connected.
Then
\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{id}}(f,V)
is
contractible
if and only if either of the following conditions is satisfied:

(i)

f
is has at least one saddle critical point or degenerate local extreme;

(ii)

M
is non-orientable;

(iii)

V
contains more than
\chi(M)
points (which holds e.g. when
\dim V\geq 1
or
\chi(M)<0
).

In all other cases
\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{id}}(f,V)
is
homotopy equivalent to the circle
S^{1}
.
In fact the latter holds if and only if
f
is one of maps of types
(A)
-
(D)
of Theorem
5.2.2
below.

The following theorem characterizes Morse maps having only local extremes and describes their
\mathbf{O}
-sequences.

Theorem 5.2.2
.

(
[
34
, Theorem 1.9]
)

Let
f\in\mathcal{F}(M,P)
.
Then every critical point of
f
is a
non-degenerate local extreme
if and only if
f
is one of the maps of the following types
(A)
-
(E)
.

(A)

f:S^{2}\to P
is a Morse map having precisely two critical points
\{a,b\}
, and those points are non-degenerate local extremes.
In this case

\displaystyle\mathbf{O}\left(f\right)\ \stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\sf\tiny{w}}}}{{\simeq}}\ [S^{1}\equiv S^{1}\lhook\joinrel\rightarrow{}SO(3)\rightarrow\!\!\!\!\!\to{}S^{2}],

\displaystyle\mathbf{O}\left(f,\{a\}\right)\ \stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\sf\tiny{w}}}}{{\simeq}}\ \mathbf{O}\left(f,\{a,b\}\right)\ \stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\sf\tiny{w}}}}{{\simeq}}\ [S^{1}\equiv S^{1}\equiv S^{1}\rightarrow\!\!\!\!\!\to{}\bullet].

(B)

f\in\mathcal{F}(D^{2},P)
is a map having precisely one critical point
a
, and that point is a non-degenerate local extreme.
In this case

\displaystyle\mathbf{O}\left(f\right)\ \stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\sf\tiny{w}}}}{{\simeq}}\ \mathbf{O}\left(f,\{a\}\right)\ \stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\sf\tiny{w}}}}{{\simeq}}\ [S^{1}\equiv S^{1}\equiv S^{1}\rightarrow\!\!\!\!\!\to{}\bullet].

(C)

f\in\mathcal{F}(S^{1}\times[0,1],P)
is a map having no critical points and

\mathbf{O}\left(f\right)\ \stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\sf\tiny{w}}}}{{\simeq}}\ [S^{1}\equiv S^{1}\equiv S^{1}\rightarrow\!\!\!\!\!\to{}\bullet].

(D)

f\in\mathcal{F}(T^{2},S^{1})
is a map without critical points.
Let
m{\mathbb{Z}}\subset{\mathbb{Z}}\cong\pi_{1}S^{1}
be the image of the induced homomorphism
f_{1}:\pi_{1}T^{2}\to\pi_{1}S^{1}
for some
m
.
Then
m\geq 1
,
f:T^{2}\to S^{1}
is a locally trivial fibration whose fiber is a disjoint union of
m
circles, and

\mathbf{O}\left(f\right)\ \stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\sf\tiny{w}}}}{{\simeq}}\ [S^{1}\times 0\lhook\joinrel\rightarrow S^{1}\times{\mathbb{Z}}_{m}\lhook\joinrel\rightarrow S^{1}\times S^{1}\xrightarrow{(w,z)\mapsto z^{m}}\!\!\!\!\!\to S^{1}]

(E)

f\in\mathcal{F}(K,S^{1})
is be a map from Klein bottle to the circle having no critical points.
Let
m{\mathbb{Z}}\subset{\mathbb{Z}}\cong\pi_{1}S^{1}
be the image of the induced homomorphism
f_{1}:\pi_{1}K\to\pi_{1}S^{1}\equiv{\mathbb{Z}}
for some
m
.
Then
m\geq 1
,
f:K\to S^{1}
is a locally trivial fibration whose fiber is a disjoint union of
m
circles, and

\mathbf{O}\left(f\right)\ \stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\sf\tiny{w}}}}{{\simeq}}\ [0\lhook\joinrel\rightarrow{\mathbb{Z}}_{m}\lhook\joinrel\rightarrow S^{1}\xrightarrow{z\mapsto z^{m}}\!\!\!\!\!\to S^{1}]

All the above weak homotopy equivalences are homotopy equivalences.

Remark 5.2.3
.

To clarify the statement notice that the first weak homotopy equivalence
\mathbf{O}\left(f\right)\ \stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\sf\tiny{w}}}}{{\simeq}}\ [S^{1}\equiv S^{1}\lhook\joinrel\rightarrow{}SO(3)\rightarrow\!\!\!\!\!\to{}S^{2}]
in
(A)
means that
\mathcal{O}_{f}(f)
is homotopy equivalent to
2
-sphere
S^{2}
, and
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(f)
is homotopy equivalent to the circle
S^{1}
.
Since it is path connected, we also have that
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(f)=\Delta^{\prime}(f)=\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{id}}(f)
.

For example, regard
S^{2}=\{x^{2}+y^{2}+z^{2}=1\}
as a unit sphere in
{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
, and let
f:S^{2}\to{\mathbb{R}}
be given by
f(x,y,z)=z
.
Then
f
is a function of type
(A)
.
Let
H:S^{2}\times[0,1]\to S^{2}
be the linear rotations given by the formula:

H(x,y,z,t)=\begin{pmatrix}\cos 2\pi t&\sin 2\pi t&0\\
-\sin 2\pi t&\cos 2\pi t&0\\
0&0&1\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}x\\
y\\
z\end{pmatrix}

Evidently, each
H_{t}
preserves coordinate
z
, i.e.
f\circ H_{t}=f
.
Moreover,
H_{0}=H_{1}=\mathrm{id}_{S^{2}}
.
In other words,
H_{t}
is a loop in
\mathcal{S}(f)
at
\mathrm{id}_{S^{2}}
.
Then the inclusion of this loop as a map
\nu:S^{1}\to\mathcal{S}(f)
,
\nu(e^{2\pi it})=H_{t}
, which turns out to be a homotopy equivalence.

Similar consideration can be applied to other cases and we refer the reader to
[
34
, Proof of Theorem 1.9]
.

Corollary 5.2.4
.

Suppose
\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{id}}(f,V)
is contractible.
Then the following statements hold.

(a)

\pi_{n}\mathcal{D}(M,V)\cong\pi_{n}\mathcal{O}_{f}(f,V)
for
n\geq 2
and we get the following short exact sequence

(36)

\pi_{1}\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{id}}(M,V)\lhook\joinrel\xrightarrow{\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathsf{p}_{1}\penalty\ \penalty\ }\pi_{1}\mathcal{O}_{f}(f,V)\xrightarrow{\penalty\ \partial_{1}\penalty\ }\!\!\!\!\!\to\pi_{0}\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(f,V).

(b)

If
M\not=S^{2}
and
{\mathbb{R}}{P}^{2}
, then
\mathcal{O}_{f}(f,V)
is aspherical
1
1

1

A path connected topological space
Q
is
aspherical
, if
\pi_{n}Q=0
for
n\geq 2
.
In this case
Q
is also called Eilenberg–MacLane space
K(\pi_{1}Q,1)
.
, so its homotopy type is determined by the fundamental group
\pi_{1}\mathcal{O}_{f}(f,V)
.
In particular,
\pi_{1}\mathcal{O}_{f}(f,V)
is torsion free.

(c)

If
\chi(M)<|V|
, then
\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{id}}(M,V)
is contractible as well, and and Eq. (
36
) yields an isomorphism

(37)

\partial_{1}:\pi_{1}\mathcal{O}_{f}(f,V)\cong\pi_{0}\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(f,V).

Then
\pi_{0}\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(f,V)
is also torsion free and this group completely determine the homotopy type of
\mathcal{O}_{f}(f,V)
.

5.3.
Structure of
\pi_{1}\mathcal{O}_{f}(f,V)

We will describe here the general structure of fundamental groups of orbits, see Theorem
5.5
and Remark
5.4.1
.

Suppose that
\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{id}}(f,V)
is
contractible
.
Then we have the following commutative diagram:

(38)

in which all horizontal and vertical sequences are exact.
In fact, the middle horizontal sequence is the last part of the above long exact sequence of
\mathsf{p}
, while the right vertical is
{\mathbf{b}^{\prime}}\!
-sequence for
(f,V)
.

Denote
A=\partial_{1}^{-1}\bigl(\pi_{0}\Delta^{\prime}(f,V)\bigr)
and consider the upper horizontal sequence:

(39)

\pi_{1}\mathcal{D}(M,V)\ \lhook\joinrel\xrightarrow{\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathsf{p}_{1}\penalty\ \penalty\ }\ A\ \xrightarrow{\penalty\ \partial_{1}\penalty\ }\!\!\!\!\!\to\ \pi_{0}\Delta^{\prime}(f,V).

We know that
\pi_{0}\Delta^{\prime}(f,V)
is a free abelian,
\pi_{1}\mathcal{D}(M,V)
is abelian (
0
,
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
,
{\mathbb{Z}}
, or
{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}
) and its image is contained in the center of
\pi_{1}\mathcal{O}_{f}(f,V)
, and therefore in the center of
A
.
One might expect that this sequence splits, i.e.
\partial_{1}
in (
39
) admits a section
q:\pi_{0}\Delta^{\prime}(f,V)\to A
, which gave an isomorphism

\phi:\pi_{1}\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{id}}(M,V)\oplus\pi_{0}\Delta^{\prime}(f,V)\cong A
,
\phi(a,b)=\mathsf{p}_{1}(a)\cdot q(b)
.
This was initially stated in
[
34
, Theorem 1.5]
for
V=\varnothing
, however the arguments contained a gap and were completed in
[
40
, Theorem 6]
.

The following theorem describes a more general effect.
Let
\mathbb{T}^{k}
be the
k
-dimensional torus, i.e. a product of
k
circles.

.

(cf.
[

, Theorem 6]
)

be any isomorphism.
Then there exists a continuous map

such that we have the following commutative diagram:
