0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern-32.16959pt\raise-20.8789pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ \pi_{1}\mathcal{D}(M,V)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ }$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 32.16959pt\raise-20.8789pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@hook{1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 52.2203pt\raise-15.6428pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-0.875pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\mathsf{p}_{1}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 85.79054pt\raise-20.8789pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 85.79054pt\raise-20.8789pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ \pi_{1}\mathcal{O}_{f}(f,V)\ \ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 156.24107pt\raise-14.94835pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.93056pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\partial_{1}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 176.02217pt\raise-20.8789pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern-3.0pt\lower 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 150.6333pt\raise-28.54099pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@hook{1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 151.4206pt\raise-38.16771pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.75pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\rho\circ\partial_{1}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 179.96954pt\raise-35.57225pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}{\hbox{\kern 176.02217pt\raise-20.8789pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ \pi_{0}\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(f,V)\ \ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 206.14317pt\raise-31.31836pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-0.8264pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\rho}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 206.14317pt\raise-33.79553pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\lower-3.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern-3.0pt\raise-41.75781pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 115.21191pt\raise-41.75781pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 179.96954pt\raise-41.75781pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ \mathcal{G}^{\prime}(f,V)\ }$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces

in which main diagonal, middle row and right column are exact.

Therefore, if
\sigma:\tilde{\mathcal{O}}\to\mathcal{O}_{f}(f,V)
is a covering map corresponding to the subgroup

\psi_{1}\bigl(\pi_{1}\bigl(\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{id}}(M,V)\times\mathbb{T}^{k}\bigr)\bigr)

of
\pi_{1}\mathcal{O}_{f}(f,V)
, then
\psi
lifts to the map

\hat{\psi}:\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{id}}(M,V)\times\mathbb{T}^{k}\to\tilde{\mathcal{O}}

such that
\psi=\sigma\circ\hat{\psi}
and
\hat{\psi}
induces an isomorphism of all homotopy groups, i.e.
\hat{\psi}
is a weak homotopy equivalence.

In particular, if
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}(f,V)
is trivial (which holds e.g. when
f
is a generic Morse map), then
\psi_{1}
is an isomorphism as well, whence
\psi
is a weak homotopy equivalence (cf.
[
34
, Theorem 1.5(3)]
).

Proof.

Let
I^{k}=[0,1]^{k}
be a
k
-dimensional cube.
Then there is a natural quotient map

\nu:I^{k}\to\mathbb{T}^{k},\qquad\nu(t_{1},\ldots,t_{k})=\bigl(e^{2\pi it_{1}},\ldots,e^{2\pi it_{k}}\bigr).

We will construct a certain continuous map
\zeta:I^{k}\to\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{id}}(M,V\cup Q)
such that for all
i=1,\ldots,k
, and
t_{1},\ldots,t_{i-1},t_{i+1},\ldots,t_{k}\in[0,1]
we will have

f\circ\zeta(t_{1},\ldots,t_{i-1},0,t_{i},\ldots,t_{k})=f\circ\zeta(t_{1},\ldots,t_{i-1},1,t_{i+1},\ldots,t_{k}).

This will imply existence of a unique continuous map
\eta:\mathbb{T}^{k}\to\mathcal{O}_{f}(f,V)
with
\mathsf{p}\circ\zeta=\eta\circ\nu
.
Moreover,
\zeta
will be constructed so that
\eta
will make commutative the diagram (
40
).

Notation.

Notice that
[0,1]
can be regarded as a CW-complex with two
0
-cells
\{0\}
and
\{1\}
and a
1
-cell
(0,1)
.
Then
I^{k}=[0,1]^{k}
inherits a CW-structure, whose cells are product of cells of copies of
[0,1]
.
It will be convenient to introduce uniform notation for such faces.

Let
K=\{1,\ldots,k\}
.
Then each
i
-th face
E
of
I^{k}
is determined by partition of
K
into three mutually disjoint subsets
A
,
B
,
C
such that

•

for each
s\in A
there is
(t_{1},\ldots,t_{k})\in E
with
0<t_{s}<1
.

•

for each
s\in B
and
(t_{1},\ldots,t_{k})\in E
we always have
t_{s}=0
;

•

for each
s\in C
and
(t_{1},\ldots,t_{k})\in E
we always have
t_{s}=1
;

Denote such face by
E_{A,\,B,\,C}
.
Evidently, the dimension of
E_{A,\,B,\,C}
equals to the number of elements of
A
.
Moreover, when writing points of
E_{A,\,B,\,C}
we will sometimes indicate only coordinates belonging to
A
.

Construction of
\zeta
.

One can easily find

(a)

a subset
Q\subset M
such that
|Q|>\chi(M)
;

(b)

pairwise commuting diffeomorphisms
\tau_{1},\ldots,\tau_{k}\in\Delta^{\prime}(f,V)
whose isotopy classes
[\tau_{i}]
constitute a basis for
\pi_{0}\Delta^{\prime}(f)\cong{\mathbb{Z}}^{k}
;

(c)

isotopies
H^{i}:M\times[0,1]\to M
relatively
Q
with
H^{i}=\mathrm{id}_{M}
and
H^{i}_{1}=\tau_{i}
for all
i=1,\ldots,k
.

Indeed, for each edge
e
of
\Gamma_{f}
choose a Dehn twist
\tau_{e}
along
e
.
Then one can assume that each
\tau_{i}
is a product of some
\tau_{e}
.
This will guarantee condition
(b)
.
Existence of
Q
and isotopies
H^{i}
as in
(a)
and
(c)
is proved in
[
40
, Theorem 6]
for
V=\varnothing
.
That sets also suffice if
V\not=\varnothing
is finite but
|V|<\chi(M)
.
E.g. if
M=D^{2}
, then one can take
Q=\partial D^{2}
.
If
|V|>\chi(M)
, then one can put
Q=V
.

Now
\zeta
will be constructed by induction on
d
-dimensional faces of
I^{k}
.

Let
d=0
.
Put

\zeta(\varepsilon_{1},\ldots,\varepsilon_{k})=\tau_{1}^{\varepsilon_{1}}\circ\cdots\circ\tau_{k}^{\varepsilon_{k}}=\prod_{i=1}^{k}\tau_{i},\qquad\varepsilon_{i}\in\{0,1\}.

Since
\tau_{i}
pairwise commute, one can write the product of
\tau_{i}
in any order, and therefore the notation
\prod_{i=1}^{k}\tau_{i}
is well-defined.
Then

f\circ\zeta(\varepsilon_{1},\ldots,\varepsilon_{k})=f\circ\tau_{1}^{\varepsilon_{1}}\circ\cdots\circ\tau_{k}^{\varepsilon_{k}}=f.

Let
d=1
.
Every
1
-dimensional cell has the form
E_{\{i\},\,B,\,C}
, and so it is ‘‘parametrized’’ by its
i
-th coordinate
t_{i}
.
Then we define
\zeta
on
E_{\{i\},\,B,\,C}
by

\zeta(t_{i})=\bigl(\prod_{j\in C}\tau_{j}\bigr)\circ H^{i}_{t_{i}}.

In other words, we first apply an isotopy
H^{i}
, and if
j
-th coordinate of the face is
1
(for
j\not=i
), then we additionally make a composition with
\tau_{j}
.
Since all
\tau_{i}
pairwise commute,
\zeta
is correctly defined on
1
-skeleton of
I^{k}
.

It follows that

f\circ\zeta(t_{i})=f\circ H^{i}_{t_{i}},\qquad t_{i}\in E_{A,\,B,\,C}.

For example, if
k=2
, then

\displaystyle\zeta(t_{1},0)

\displaystyle=H^{1}_{t},

\displaystyle\zeta(t_{1},1)

\displaystyle=\tau_{2}\circ H^{1}_{t},

\displaystyle\zeta(0,t_{2})

\displaystyle=H^{2}_{t},

\displaystyle\zeta(1,t_{2})

\displaystyle=\tau_{1}\circ H^{2}_{t}.

Let
d=2
.
Let
i_{1}<i_{2}
be two distinct indices and

E=E_{\{i_{1},i_{2}\},\,K\setminus\{i_{1},i_{2}\},\,\varnothing}

be a
2
-face of
I^{k}
in which all coordinates except for
i_{1}
and
i_{2}
are zeros.

By the construction
\zeta(\partial E)\in\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{id}}(M,V\cup Q)
, and the latter space is contractible.
Hence
\zeta
extends to some continuous map

H^{i_{1},i_{2}}:E\to\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{id}}(M,V\cup Q).

Extend
\zeta
to each other
2
-face
E_{\{i_{1},i_{2}\},\,B,\,C}
spanned by coordinates
i_{1},i_{2}
in a similar way:

\zeta(t_{i_{1}},t_{i_{2}})=\bigl(\prod_{j\in C}\tau_{j}\bigr)\circ H^{i_{1},i_{2}}(t_{i_{1}},t_{i_{2}}).

Then again

f\circ\zeta(t_{i_{1}},t_{i_{2}})=f\circ H^{i_{1},i_{2}}(t_{i_{1}},t_{i_{2}})

for any
2
-face
E_{\{i_{1},i_{2}\},\,B,\,C}
.

Let
d=3
.
The construction is similar to the case
d=2
.
Let
i_{1}<i_{2}<i_{3}
be three distinct indices and

E=E_{\{i_{1},i_{2},i_{3}\},\,K\setminus\{i_{1},i_{2},i_{3}\},\,\varnothing}

be a
3
-face of
I^{k}
in which all coordinates except for
i_{1}
,
i_{2}
, and
i_{3}
are zeros.

By the construction
\zeta(\partial E)\in\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{id}}(M,V\cup Q)
, and the latter space is contractible.
Hence
\zeta
extends to some continuous map

H^{i_{1},i_{2},i_{3}}:E\to\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{id}}(M,V\cup Q)

and we extend
\zeta
to each other
3
-face
E_{\{i_{1},i_{2},i_{3}\},\,B,\,C}
spanned by coordinates
i_{1},i_{2},i_{3}
in a similar way:

\zeta(t_{i_{1}},t_{i_{2}},t_{i_{3}})=\bigl(\prod_{j\in C}\tau_{j}\bigr)\circ H^{i_{1},i_{2},i_{3}}(t_{i_{1}},t_{i_{2}},t_{i_{3}}).

Applying the same arguments so on, we extend
\zeta
to all of
I^{k}
.

Verification of properties of
\eta
.

By construction the restriction of
\zeta
to each
1
-face spanned by coordinate
t_{i}
, is given by the loop
\gamma_{i}:=f\circ H^{i}
.
But due to description of
\partial_{1}
(see Remark
5.1.2
),
\partial_{1}([\gamma_{i}])=H^{i}_{1}=\tau_{i}
.
This means commutativity of (
40
).
∎

It is convenient to formulate the general observation about the homotopy types of
\mathcal{O}(f,V)
in the form of the following property
(Bib)
.

Definition 5.4
.

Let
M
be a compact surface,
f\in\mathcal{F}(M,P)
,
V
be an
f
-adapted submanifold, and
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}(f,V)
be the group of automorphisms of the graph of
f
induced by elements of
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(f,V)
, see (
28
).
Say that a pair
(f,V)
has property
(Bib)
whenever

(Bib)

there exists a
free
action of the (finite) group
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}(f,V)
on a
k
-torus
\mathbb{T}^{k}
for some
k\geq 0
such that we have the following homotopy equivalence:

\mathcal{O}_{f}(f,V)\,\simeq\,\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{id}}(M,V)\times\bigl(\mathbb{T}^{k}/\mathcal{G}^{\prime}(f,V)\bigr),

where
\mathbb{T}^{k}/\mathcal{G}^{\prime}(f,V)
is the corresponding quotient space.

Remark 5.4.1
.

Evidently, Property
(Bib)
generalizes the effect described in Theorem
5.3.1
for the case when
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}(f,V)
is trivial.
That property was discovered by E. Kudryavtseva in a series of papers
[
23
,
24
,
26
]
, in which she described the homotopy structure of the spaces
\mathcal{F}_{p,q,r}
of Morse functions on orientable compact surfaces equipped with a numeration of their critical points (such spaces are finite covering spaces of the spaces of all Morse functions).
She presented a ‘‘finite-dimensional’’ model for the stratifications of those spaces into orbits of Morse functions, and demonstrated Property
(Bib)
for them.

Remark 5.4.2
.

Notice that not all pairs
(f,V)
have property
(Bib)
.
For instance, if
f:S^{2}\to{\mathbb{R}}
is a Morse function with two only critical points as in Theorem
5.2.1
(A)
, then
\pi_{1}\mathcal{O}_{f}(f)=\pi_{1}S^{2}=0
.
On the other hand, the graph of
f
is a closed segment, whence
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}(f)
is trivial, and therefore

\pi_{1}\bigl(\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{id}}(S^{2})\times(\mathbb{T}^{k}/\mathcal{G}^{\prime}(f))\bigr)\cong{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}\times{\mathbb{Z}}^{k}\not=0\ \text{for any $k\geq 0$}.

Nevertheless this property seems to be very typical.
The following theorem describes several cases when
(Bib)
holds.

Theorem 5.5
.

Let
M
be a compact connected surface,
f\in\mathcal{F}(M,P)
, and
V
be an
f
-adapted submanifold.
Then the pair
(f,V)
has property
(Bib)
in the following cases.

(1)

\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{id}}(f,V)
is contractible, and
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}(f,V)
is trivial
(Theorem
5.3.1
)
.

(2)

M
is orientable,
V=\varnothing
, and
f\in\mathcal{M}(M,{\mathbb{R}})
is such that the Euler characteristic
\chi(M)
of
M
is
less than
the total number of fixed critical points of
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(f)

(E. Kudryavtseva
[
23
,
24
,
26
]
)
.

Warning.

In
[
44
, Corollary 1.3 & Theorem 5.10]
it was claimed that Property
(Bib)
holds for pairs
(f,V)
admitting a diagonal short exact sequence from (
41
).
However, such a statement is not in fact proved.
The reason is that the above claims were just direct applications of
[
44
, Theorem 2.5]
being in turn an
incorrect
formulation of a realization theorem for crystallographic groups.
That
[
44
, Theorem 2.5]
states that for
every
short exact sequence
{\mathbb{Z}}^{k}\lhook\joinrel\rightarrow\Gamma\rightarrow\!\!\!\!\!\to G
with finite
G
and torsion free
\Gamma
, there exists a free action of
G
on
\mathbb{T}^{k}
such that
\pi_{1}(\mathbb{T}^{k}/G)\cong\Gamma
.
In fact, in general one should additionally require that
{\mathbb{Z}}^{k}
is a
maximal abelian subgroup of
\Gamma
(this is a theorem by H. Zassenhaus, see e.g.
[
53
, Theorem 2.2]
).

Nevertheless this does not mean that
[
44
, Corollary 1.3 & Theorem 5.10]
are wrong.
Indeed, consider the following short exact sequence
\mathbf{z}_{n}:n{\mathbb{Z}}\lhook\joinrel\rightarrow{\mathbb{Z}}\rightarrow\!\!\!\!\!\to{\mathbb{Z}}_{n}
.
Then
n{\mathbb{Z}}
is not a maximal abelian subgroup of
{\mathbb{Z}}
, however there exists an action of
{\mathbb{Z}}_{n}
on
\mathbb{T}^{1}=S^{1}
such that
\pi_{1}(\mathbb{T}^{1}/{\mathbb{Z}}_{3})\cong{\mathbb{Z}}
.

Therefore to complete the proofs of
[
44
, Corollary 1.3 & Theorem 5.10]
one needs to thoroughly check conditions on
(f,V)
when such actions of
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}(f,V)
on some tori exist.
This will be done in another paper.
∎

In next sections we will describe algebraic structure of
\pi_{1}\mathcal{O}_{f}(f,V)
for the case when
M
is orientable and distinct from
S^{2}
.

6.
Maps on surfaces with
\chi(M)<0

Let
M
be compact surface,
f\in\mathcal{F}(M,P)
, and
V
an
f
-adapted submanifold.
In this section we give a complete description of algebraic structure of Bieberbach sequence (
25
):

\mathbf{b}(f,V):\quad\pi_{0}\Delta(f,V)\lhook\joinrel\rightarrow\pi_{0}\mathcal{S}(f,V)\rightarrow\!\!\!\!\!\to\pi_{0}\mathcal{G}(f,V).

for the case when
M
is orientable and differs from
S^{2}
.

6.1.
Reduction to the case
\chi(M)\geq 0

Theorem
6.1.1
below shows that computations of
\mathbf{b}^{\prime}(f,V)
completely reduces to the case when
\chi(M)\geq 0
(i.e. is one of the following surfaces:
S^{2}
,
T^{2}
,
D^{2}
,
S^{1}\times[0,1]
,
{\mathbb{R}}{P}^{2}
, Möbius band and Klein bottle) and
V
consists of critical points of
f
and boundary components of
M
.

Let
R_{V}
be a
f
-regular neighborhood of
V
and
D_{1},\ldots,D_{q}
all the connected components of the closure
\overline{M\setminus R_{V}}
which are diffeomorphic with a
2
-disk.
Then the union

N_{V}:=R_{V}\cup D_{1}\cup\ldots\cup D_{q}

is called a
canonical neighborhood

N_{V}
of
V
(corresponding to
R_{V}
), see
[
17
]
.

Theorem 6.1.1
.

(
[
39
]
,
[
44
, Theorem 5.4]
)

Suppose
M
is connected with
\chi(M)<0
.
Let also
K
be the union of all non-extremal critical leaves of
f

whose canonical neighborhoods have negative Euler characteristic
,
R_{K}
an
f
-regular neighborhood of
K
,
B_{1},\ldots,B_{k}
all the connected components of
\overline{M\setminus R_{K}}
, and
{X}^{\partial}_{i}:=B_{i}\cap(V\cup R_{K})
,
i=1,\ldots,k
.
Then

(1)

each
B_{i}
is diffeomorphic either with a
2
-disk or a cylinder or a Möbius band;

(2)

the inclusion
\bigl(\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(f,V\cup R_{K}),\Delta^{\prime}(f,V\cup R_{K})\bigr)\subset\bigr(\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(f,V),\Delta^{\prime}(f,V)\bigr)
is a homotopy equivalence, inducing therefore an isomorphism

\mathbf{b}^{\prime}(f,V)\cong\mathbf{b}^{\prime}(f,V\cup R_{K})\cong\mathop{\prod}\limits_{i=1}^{k}\mathbf{b}^{\prime}(f|_{B_{i}},{X}^{\partial}_{i}).

6.2.
Relation between sequences
\mathbf{b}^{\prime}(f,V)
and
\mathbf{b}(f)

Denote by

j_{0}:\pi_{0}\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(f,V)\to\pi_{0}\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(f)

the homomorphism induced by the inclusion
j:\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(f,V)\subset\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(f)
.

Lemma 6.2.1

(​​
[
44
, Lemma 5.1]
)
.

Suppose
M
is connected and
V
is a
non-empty
union of several boundary components of
M
.
Suppose also that
\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{id}}(f)
is contractible.

(i)

Then we have the following commutative diagram:

(42)

\displaystyle\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.36113pt\hbox{\kern 23.3934pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&&\cr&&&\cr&&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-23.3934pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise Since the groups
\pi_{0}\Delta^{\prime}(f,V)
and
\pi_{0}\Delta^{\prime}(f)
are free abelian, the left column splits, so there is a section

\xi:\pi_{0}\Delta^{\prime}(f)\to\pi_{0}\Delta^{\prime}(f,V)
.

(iii)

If
\chi(M)<0
, and thus
\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{id}}(M)
is contractible, then by (
42
),
j_{0}
is an isomorphism, and (
43
) yields an isomorphism
\mathbf{b}^{\prime}(f,V)\cong\mathbf{b}^{\prime}(f)
.

(iv)

If
\chi(M)\geq 0
, so
M
is either a
2
-disk, or a cylinder, or a Möbius band (since
\partial M\not=\varnothing
), then
\ker(j_{0})\cong\pi_{1}\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{id}}(M)\cong{\mathbb{Z}}
due to (
42
), and the diagram (
43
) is a short exact sequence
\mathbf{z}_{1}\lhook\joinrel\rightarrow\mathbf{b}^{\prime}(f,V)\rightarrow\!\!\!\!\!\to\mathbf{b}^{\prime}(f)
.

In fact, for
M
being a
2
-disk of a Möbius band, then
\ker(j_{0})
is generated by a Dehn twist along boundary component of
M
, and for
M=S^{1}\times[0,1]
the kernel
\ker(j_{0})
is generated by a pair of Dehn twists in different directions along boundary components of
M
.

In the next two sections we will describe the algebraic structure of
\mathbf{b}(f,V)
for the cases when
M
is a
2
-disk, cylinder and torus.
Together with Theorem
6.1.1
this will give complete information on
\mathbf{b}(f,V)
for all orientable surfaces distinct from
S^{2}
.

7.
Maps on
2
-disk and cylinder

Suppose
(M,V)
is one of the pairs
(D^{2},\partial D^{2})
or
(S^{1}\times[0,1],S^{1}\times 0)
. Then the group
\mathcal{D}(M,V)
is connected (in fact even contractible, see Table
1
).
Hence
\mathcal{D}(M,V)=\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{id}}(M,V)
and

(44)

\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(f,V)=\mathcal{S}(f,V)\cap\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{id}}(M,V)=\mathcal{S}(f,V)\cap\mathcal{D}(M,V)=\mathcal{S}(f,V).

Similarly,
\Delta^{\prime}(f,V)=\Delta(f,V)
.
This implies that
\mathbf{b}(f,V)=\mathbf{b}^{\prime}(f,V)
.

Recall that if
\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{id}}(f)
is contractible, then by Lemma
6.2.1
(iv)
we also have the following short exact sequence:

(45)

\mathbf{z}_{1}\lhook\joinrel\rightarrow\mathbf{b}^{\prime}(f,V)\rightarrow\!\!\!\!\!\to\mathbf{b}^{\prime}(f)

which is the same as the diagram (
43
).

7.1.
Maps with minimal number of critical points

Theorem 7.1.1
.

(
[
44
, Theorem 5.5]
)

Let
(M,V)=(S^{1}\times[0,1],S^{1}\times 0)
.

(1)

For each
f\in{\mathcal{F}}(M,P)
the inclusion of pairs

\bigl(\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(f,\partial M),\,\Delta^{\prime}(f,\partial M)\bigr)\subset\bigl(\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(f,V),\,\Delta^{\prime}(f,V)\bigr)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{equ:StabIsotIdD2}}}{{\equiv}}\bigl(\mathcal{S}(f,V),\,\Delta(f,V)\bigr)

is a homotopy equivalence, whence
\mathbf{b}^{\prime}(f,\partial M)\cong\mathbf{b}^{\prime}(f,V)\equiv\mathbf{b}(f,V)
.

(2)

If
f\in{\mathcal{F}}(M,P)
has
no critical points
, then

\displaystyle\pi_{0}\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(f,V)

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle\pi_{0}\mathcal{S}(f,\partial M)

\displaystyle={\mathbb{Z}}.

In particular,
{\mathbf{b}^{\prime}}\!\left(f,V\right)\cong\mathbf{z}_{0}:\{1\}\lhook\joinrel\rightarrow\{1\}\rightarrow\!\!\!\!\!\to\{1\}
.

Theorem 7.1.2
.

(
[
44
, Theorem 5.6]
)

Suppose
f\in{\mathcal{F}}(D^{2},P)
has a
unique critical point

z
being therefore a
local extreme
.

(1)

If
z
is
non-degenerate
, then

{\mathbf{b}^{\prime}}\!\left(f,\partial D^{2}\right)\cong\mathbf{z}_{0}:\{1\}\lhook\joinrel\rightarrow\{1\}\rightarrow\!\!\!\!\!\to\{1\}
.

(2)

If
z
is
degenerate
of symmetry index
m
, then
\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{id}}(f)
is contractible, and the sequence (
45
) is isomorphic with
(3\times 3)
-diagram
\natural\mathbf{z}_{m}
, see (
16
):

\textstyle{\mathbf{z}_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\hskip-28.45274pt:}
\textstyle{m{\mathbb{Z}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{m{\mathbb{Z}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathbf{b}^{\prime}(f,\partial D^{2})\cong\mathbf{z}_{m}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\hskip-28.45274pt:}
\textstyle{m{\mathbb{Z}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\mathbb{Z}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\mathbb{Z}}_{m}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathbf{b}^{\prime}(f)}
\textstyle{\hskip-28.45274pt:}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\mathbb{Z}}_{m}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\mathbb{Z}}_{m}}

7.2.
General case

It follows from these theorems that

(46)

\mathbf{b}(f,V)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{equ:StabIsotIdD2}}}{{\cong}}\mathbf{b}^{\prime}(f,V)\cong\mathbf{b}^{\prime}(f,\partial M),

where the second and third sequences coincide if
M=D^{2}
and are isomorphic for
M=S^{1}\times[0,1]
by Theorem
7.1.1
(1)
.
Our aim is to compute the sequence (
46
).

Let
K
be a unique critical leaf of
f
such that the connected component of
M\setminus K
containing
V
includes no critical points of
f
. Equivalently, let
v
be the vertex of
\Gamma_{f}
of
f
corresponding to
V
.
Then
v
belongs to a unique edge
e
of
\Gamma_{f}
, and
K
is the leaf of
f
corresponding to another vertex of
e
.

It follows from uniqueness of
K
that

(47)

h(K)=K,\qquad\forall\ h\in\mathcal{S}(f,V).

Let
c=f(K)\in P
and
\varepsilon>0
.
Denote by
R_{K}
the connected component of
f^{-1}[c-\varepsilon,c+\varepsilon]
containing
K
.
Decreasing
\varepsilon
we can assume that
R_{K}\setminus K
contains no critical points of
f
and
R_{K}\cap\partial M\subset\partial R_{K}
(this intersection may be empty).
In particular,
R_{K}
is an
f
-regular neighborhood of
K
.

Let
\mathbf{Z}
be the collection of all connected components of
\overline{M\setminus R_{K}}
.
Since by (
47
)
K
is
\mathcal{S}(f,V)
-invariant, we have that so are
R_{K}
and
\overline{M\setminus R_{K}}
.
In other words, we get a natural action of
\mathcal{S}(f,V)
on
\mathbf{Z}
.
Let

(48)

\mathcal{S}(\mathbf{Z})=\{h\in\mathcal{S}(f,V)\mid h(Z)=Z\ \text{for each}\ Z\in\mathbf{Z}\}

be its kernel of non-effectiveness.
Then the quotient
\mathcal{S}(f,V)/\mathcal{S}(\mathbf{Z})

effectively
acts on
\mathbf{Z}
.

\bullet

Let
\mathbf{Z}^{fix}=\{X_{0},X_{1},\ldots,X_{a}\}
be all the elements of
\mathbf{Z}
invariant under all diffeomorphisms from
\mathcal{S}(f,V)
, i.e. fixed points of
\mathcal{S}(f,V)/\mathcal{S}(\mathbf{Z})
. Enumerate them so that
V\subset X_{0}
, and in particular,
X_{0}
is always a cylinder.
Evidently, if
M=D^{2}
, them all
X_{i}
,
i\geq 1
, are
2
-disks.
On the other hand, if
M=S^{1}\times[0,1]
, then the element of
\mathbf{Z}
containing another boundary component
S^{1}\times 1
is invariant under
\mathcal{S}(f,V)
and we will always denote it by
X_{1}
.
In this case
X_{1}
is a cylinder, and all others
X_{i}
,
i\geq 2
, must be
2
-disks.

\bullet

Let also
\mathbf{Z}^{reg}:=\mathbf{Z}\setminus\mathbf{Z}^{fix}=\{Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{b}\}
be all other
2
-disks of
\mathbf{Z}
.
Thus each
Y_{i}
is not invariant under some element of
\mathcal{S}(f,V)
.

The following theorem expresses
\mathbf{b}^{\prime}(f,\partial M)
via the corresponding sequences
\{\mathbf{b}^{\prime}(f|_{Z},\partial Z)\}_{Z\in\mathbf{Z}}
.
It is essentially based in Lemma
2.9.1
,

Theorem 7.2.1
.

(A)

Suppose that all elements of
\mathbf{Z}
are invariant under
\mathcal{S}(f,V)
, which is equivalent to either of the following three conditions:

\displaystyle\mathbf{Z}^{reg}

\displaystyle=\varnothing,

\displaystyle\mathbf{Z}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{Z}^{fix}=\{X_{0},X_{1},\ldots,X_{a}\},

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}(f,V)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{S}(\mathbf{Z}).

Then
\mathbf{b}^{\prime}(f)\cong\mathop{\Pi}\limits_{i=1}^{a}\mathbf{b}^{\prime}(f|_{X_{i}},\partial X_{i}):

(49)

\mathop{\Pi}\limits_{i=1}^{a}\Delta^{\prime}(f|_{X_{i}},\partial X_{i})\ \lhook\joinrel\rightarrow\ \mathop{\Pi}\limits_{i=1}^{a}\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(f|_{X_{i}},\partial X_{i})\ \rightarrow\!\!\!\!\!\to\ \mathop{\Pi}\limits_{i=1}^{a}\mathcal{G}^{\prime}(f|_{X_{i}},\partial X_{i}),

and the sequence (
45
):
\mathbf{z}_{1}\lhook\joinrel\rightarrow\mathbf{b}^{\prime}(f,V)\rightarrow\!\!\!\!\!\to\mathbf{b}^{\prime}(f)
is isomorphic with a split sequence (
2.8.3
):

\mathbf{z}_{1}\lhook\joinrel\rightarrow\mathbf{z}_{1}\times\mathbf{b}^{\prime}(f)\rightarrow\!\!\!\!\!\to\mathbf{b}^{\prime}(f)
.
In particular,

\mathbf{b}^{\prime}(f,V)\cong\mathbf{z}_{1}\times\mathbf{b}^{\prime}(f)\cong\mathbf{b}^{\prime}(f)\times\mathbf{z}_{1}\cong\mathbf{b}^{\prime}(f)\wr\mathbf{z}_{1}:\\
\phantom{AAA}\bigl(\mathop{\Pi}\limits_{i=1}^{a}\Delta^{\prime}(f|_{X_{i}},\partial X_{i})\bigr)\times{\mathbb{Z}}\lhook\joinrel\rightarrow\bigl(\mathop{\Pi}\limits_{i=1}^{a}\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(f|_{X_{i}},\partial X_{i})\bigr)\times{\mathbb{Z}}\rightarrow\!\!\!\!\!\to\mathop{\Pi}\limits_{i=1}^{a}\mathcal{G}^{\prime}(f|_{X_{i}},\partial X_{i}),

(B)

Assume that
\mathbf{Z}^{reg}=\{Y_{i}\}_{i=1}^{b}\not=\varnothing
.

(a)

Then
\mathcal{S}(f,V)/\mathcal{S}(\mathbf{Z})\cong{\mathbb{Z}}_{m}
for some
m\geq 2
;

(b)

The
effective
action of
\mathcal{S}(f,V)/\mathcal{S}(\mathbf{Z})
on
\mathbf{Z}
is
semifree
, i.e.​ free on the set
\mathbf{Z}^{reg}
of non-fixed elements, and
has exactly either
one
or
two
fixed elements.

In particular,
m
divides
b
and that action has exactly
c:=b/m
orbits.
Fix any (
2
-disks)
Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{c}\in\mathbf{Z}^{reg}
belonging to mutually distinct non-fixed
{\mathbb{Z}}_{m}
-orbits and define the following short exact sequence:

(50)

\displaystyle\mathbf{q}

\displaystyle:=\bigl(\mathop{\prod}\limits_{j=1}^{c}\mathbf{b}^{\prime}(f|_{Y_{i}},\partial Y_{i})\bigr)\wr\mathbf{z}_{m}:

\displaystyle\text{\resizebox{21479355}{}{$\Bigl(\mathop{\Pi}\limits_{i=1}^{a}\Delta^{\prime}(f|_{X_{i}},\partial X_{i})\Bigr)^{m}\times{\mathbb{Z}}^{m}\lhook\joinrel\rightarrow\Bigl(\mathop{\Pi}\limits_{i=1}^{a}\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(f|_{X_{i}},\partial X_{i})\Bigr)\mathop{\wr}\limits_{m}{\mathbb{Z}}\rightarrow\!\!\!\!\!\to\mathop{\Pi}\limits_{i=1}^{a}\mathcal{G}^{\prime}(f|_{X_{i}},\partial X_{i})$}}.

Then the following statements hold.

(c)

If
\mathcal{S}(f,V)/\mathcal{S}(\mathbf{Z})
has a unique fixed element, i.e.
\mathbf{Z}^{fix}=\{X_{0}\}
, then

\mathbf{b}^{\prime}(f,\partial M)\cong\mathbf{q}.

(d)

Otherwise, due to
(B)(b)
,
\mathcal{S}(f,V)/\mathcal{S}(\mathbf{Z})
has exactly two fixed elements, so
\mathbf{Z}^{fix}=\{X_{0},X_{1}\}
.
Let
A_{1}=\overline{M\setminus X_{1}}
.
Then

\mathbf{Z}^{fix}_{A_{1}}=\mathbf{Z}^{fix}\setminus\{X_{1}\}=\{X_{0}\},

whence the restriction
f|_{A_{1}}
satisfies condition
(B)
(B)(c)
.
Therefore

\displaystyle\mathbf{b}^{\prime}(f|_{A_{1}},\partial A_{1})

\displaystyle\cong\mathbf{q},

\displaystyle\mathbf{b}^{\prime}(f,\partial M)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{equ:bseq_two_cyl}}}{{\cong}}\mathbf{b}^{\prime}(f|_{X_{1}},\partial X_{1})\times\mathbf{q}.

Corollary 7.2.2

(Decomposition into annuli and possibly one disk)
.

Let
M
be either a
2
-disk or a cylinder and
f\in{\mathcal{F}}(M,P)
.
Then there are
f
-adapted subsurfaces
A_{1},\ldots,A_{n}\subset M
having the following properties.

(a)

For
i=1,\ldots,n-1
the surface
A_{i}
is a
cylinder
, while
A_{n}
is either a
2
-disk or a cylinder.
Moreover, the intersection
A_{i}\cap A_{j}
for
i<j
is non-empty only for
j=i+1
and in this case it is a common boundary component of these subsurfaces.
Also
A_{1}
contains some boundary component of
M
.

(b)

For each
i=1,\ldots,n-1
there exist
m_{i},c_{i}\geq 1
and certain
f
-adapted mutually disjoint
2
-disks

Y_{i,1},\ldots,Y_{i,c_{i}}\subset A_{i}
, such that we have an isomorphism

(51)

\mathbf{b}^{\prime}(f|_{A_{i}},\partial A_{i})\cong\biggl(\mathop{\prod}\limits_{j=1}^{c_{i}}\mathbf{b}^{\prime}(f|_{Y_{i,j}},\partial Y_{i,j})\biggr)\wr\mathbf{z}_{m_{i}},

while the last sequence
\mathbf{b}^{\prime}(f|_{A_{n}},\partial A_{n})
is isomorphic either with a sequence of type (
51
) or with
\mathbf{z}_{0}
or with
\mathbf{z}_{m_{n}}
for some
m_{n}\geq 1
.

(c)

\mathbf{b}^{\prime}(f,\partial M)\cong\prod\limits_{i=1}^{n}\mathbf{b}^{\prime}(f|_{A_{i}},\partial A_{i})
.

(d)

The sequence (
45
):
\mathbf{z}_{1}\lhook\joinrel\rightarrow\mathbf{b}^{\prime}(f,\partial M)\rightarrow\!\!\!\!\!\to\mathbf{b}^{\prime}(f)
is isomorphic with diagonal Garside sequence (
15
) for the sequences
\{\mathbf{b}^{\prime}(f|_{A_{i}},\partial A_{i})\}_{i=1}^{n}
.

In order to formulate the general result about the structure of
\mathbf{b}^{\prime}(f,\partial M)
it will be convenient to introduce a series of classes of groups and short exact sequences, see Theorem
7.3.4
.

7.3.
Classes of groups and short exact sequences

A group
B
is called
crystallographic
if it admits a short exact sequence:
A\lhook\joinrel\rightarrow B\rightarrow\!\!\!\!\!\to C
in which
C
is finite and
A
is a maximal abelian subgroup of
B
being also free abelian.
A crystallographic group
B
is
Bieberbach
if it is torsion free (i.e. has no elements of finite order).

A short exact sequence
\mathbf{u}:A\lhook\joinrel\rightarrow B\rightarrow\!\!\!\!\!\to C
will be called
nearly crystallographic
if
A\in\mathcal{Z}
, i.e. it is a free abelian and
C
is finite.
In this case we also say that
\mathbf{u}
is
crystallographic
, if
A
is a maximal subgroup of
B
.
Moreover, a (nearly) crystallographic sequence
\mathbf{u}:A\lhook\joinrel\rightarrow B\rightarrow\!\!\!\!\!\to C
will be called
(nearly) Bieberbach
if
B
is torsion free.

For example, for
m\geq 2
the sequence
\mathbf{z}_{m}:m{\mathbb{Z}}\lhook\joinrel\rightarrow{\mathbb{Z}}\rightarrow\!\!\!\!\!\to{\mathbb{Z}}_{m}
is
nearly Bieberbach
but
not Bieberbach
, since
m{\mathbb{Z}}
is not a maximal subgroup of
{\mathbb{Z}}
, though the group
{\mathbb{Z}}
is Bieberbach.

A group
G
is
solvable
if it has a finite increasing sequence of subgroups
1=G_{0}\vartriangleleft G_{1}\vartriangleleft\cdots\vartriangleleft G_{n}=G
such that
G_{i}
is normal in
G_{i+1}
and each quotient
G_{i+1}/G_{i}
is abelian.
It is well known and is easy to check that in a short exact sequence of groups
A\lhook\joinrel\rightarrow B\rightarrow\!\!\!\!\!\to C
if any two groups are solvable, then so is the third one.

7.3.1.
Classes of groups

Consider the following classes of groups.

\bullet

Let
\mathcal{Z}=\{{\mathbb{Z}}^{n}\mid n\geq 0\}
be the set of all finitely generated free abelian groups.

\bullet

Let
\mathcal{B}
be the
minimal
set of isomorphism classes of groups such that
\{1\}\in\mathcal{B}
and if
A,B\in\mathcal{B}
and
m\geq 1
, then
A\times B
and
A\mathop{\wr}\limits_{m}{\mathbb{Z}}\in\mathcal{B}
as well.

\bullet

Let
\mathcal{B}_{2}
be the
minimal
set of isomorphism classes of groups such that
\{1\}\in\mathcal{B}_{2}
and if
A,B\in\mathcal{B}_{2}
then
A\times B
and
A\mathop{\wr}\limits_{2}{\mathbb{Z}}\in\mathcal{B}
.

\bullet

Let also
\mathcal{P}
be the
minimal
set of isomorphism classes of groups such that
\{1\}\in\mathcal{P}
, and if
A,B\in\mathcal{P}
and
m\geq 1
then
A\times B
,
A\wr{\mathbb{Z}}_{m}\in\mathcal{P}
as well.

\bullet

Let
\mathcal{P}_{2}
be the
minimal
set of isomorphism classes of groups such that
\{1\}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}
, and if
A,B\in\mathcal{P}
then
A\times B
,
A\wr{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}
.

Evidently,
\mathcal{B}_{2}\subset\mathcal{B}
and
\mathcal{P}_{2}\subset\mathcal{P}
.

It is easy to check that a group
G\in\mathcal{B}
(resp.
\mathcal{B}_{2}
,
\mathcal{P}
,
\mathcal{P}_{2}
) if
G
is obtained from the unit group by finitely many operations of direct products and wreath products of the form
\cdot\mathop{\wr}\limits_{m}{\mathbb{Z}}
for some
m\geq 1
(resp.
\cdot\mathop{\wr}\limits_{2}{\mathbb{Z}}
only,
\cdot\wr{\mathbb{Z}}_{m}
for some
m\geq 1
,
\cdot\wr{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
only).
For instance,

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}\

\displaystyle\ni\{1\},\quad{\mathbb{Z}}\cong\{1\}\mathop{\wr}\limits_{m}{\mathbb{Z}},\quad{\mathbb{Z}}^{m},\quad(({\mathbb{Z}}^{3}\mathop{\wr}\limits_{2}{\mathbb{Z}})\mathop{\wr}\limits_{m}{\mathbb{Z}})\times({\mathbb{Z}}\mathop{\wr}\limits_{18}{\mathbb{Z}}),

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}_{2}\

\displaystyle\ni\{1\},\quad{\mathbb{Z}}^{m},\quad(({\mathbb{Z}}^{3}\mathop{\wr}\limits_{2}{\mathbb{Z}})\mathop{\wr}\limits_{2}{\mathbb{Z}})\times({\mathbb{Z}}\mathop{\wr}\limits_{2}{\mathbb{Z}}),

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}\

\displaystyle\ni\{1\},\quad{\mathbb{Z}}_{m},\quad(({\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{6}\wr{\mathbb{Z}}_{8})\wr{\mathbb{Z}}_{11})\times({\mathbb{Z}}_{3}\wr{\mathbb{Z}}_{15}),

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{2}\

\displaystyle\ni\{1\},\quad{\mathbb{Z}}_{2},\quad(({\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{6}\wr{\mathbb{Z}}_{2})\wr{\mathbb{Z}}_{2})\times({\mathbb{Z}}_{2}\wr{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}).

7.3.2.
Classes of short exact sequences

\bullet

Let
\widetilde{\mathcal{Z}\mathcal{B}\mathcal{P}}
be the set of isomorphism classes of all
nearly crystallographic sequences
A\lhook\joinrel\rightarrow B\rightarrow\!\!\!\!\!\to C
, so
A\in\mathcal{Z}
,
B\in\mathcal{B}
, and
C\in\mathcal{P}
.

\bullet

Let
\mathcal{ZZI}=\{(\mathbf{z}_{1})^{m}:{\mathbb{Z}}^{m}={\mathbb{Z}}^{m}\rightarrow\!\!\!\!\!\to 1\}_{m\geq 0}
.

\bullet

Let also
\mathcal{ZBP}
be the
minimal
set of isomorphism classes of
short exact sequences
such that

\mathbf{z}_{0}\in\mathcal{ZBP}
, and if
\mathbf{q},\mathbf{q}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{ZBP}
and
m\in{\mathbb{N}}
then
\mathbf{q}\times\mathbf{q}^{\prime}
and
\mathbf{q}\wr\mathbf{z}_{m}\in\mathcal{ZBP}
as well.

\bullet

Let also
\mathcal{ZBP}_{2}
be the
minimal
set of isomorphism classes of
short exact sequences
such that

\mathbf{z}_{0}\in\mathcal{ZBP}_{2}
, and if
\mathbf{q},\mathbf{q}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{ZBP}_{2}
then
\mathbf{q}\times\mathbf{q}^{\prime}
and
\mathbf{q}\wr\mathbf{z}_{2}\in\mathcal{ZBP}_{2}
as well.

Similarly, a short exact sequence
\mathbf{q}
belongs to
\mathcal{ZBP}
(resp.
\mathcal{ZBP}_{2}
) iff it can be obtained from
\mathbf{z}_{0}
by finitely many operations of products of sequences and wreath products of the form
\cdot\wr\mathbf{z}_{m}
,
m\in{\mathbb{N}}
, (resp
m=2
only).

The following lemma describes properties of the above classes.

Lemma 7.3.3
.

(
[
44
, Lemma 2.6]
)

(1)

Every
B\in\mathcal{B}
is solvable and nearly Bieberbach, and every
C\in\mathcal{P}
is solvable and finite.

(2)

\mathbf{z}_{m}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{Z}\mathcal{B}\mathcal{P}}
for all
m\geq 0
.

(3)

If
\mathbf{q}_{i}:{\mathbb{Z}}^{k_{i}}\lhook\joinrel\rightarrow B_{i}\rightarrow\!\!\!\!\!\to C_{i}
,
i=1,2
, are (nearly) crystallographic (resp. (nearly) Bieberbach) and
m\geq 1
, then so are

\displaystyle\mathbf{q}_{1}\times\mathbf{q}_{2}

\displaystyle:{\mathbb{Z}}^{k_{1}+k_{2}}\lhook\joinrel\rightarrow B_{1}\!\times\!B_{2}\rightarrow\!\!\!\!\!\to C_{1}\!\times\!C_{2},

\displaystyle\mathbf{q}_{1}\wr\mathbf{z}_{m}

\displaystyle:{\mathbb{Z}}^{k_{1}m}\!\times\!m{\mathbb{Z}}\lhook\joinrel\rightarrow B_{1}\mathop{\wr}\limits_{m}{\mathbb{Z}}\rightarrow\!\!\!\!\!\to C_{1}\wr{\mathbb{Z}}_{m}.

(4)

\mathcal{ZBP}_{2}\subset\mathcal{ZBP}\subset\widetilde{\mathcal{Z}\mathcal{B}\mathcal{P}}
.

Theorem 7.3.4
.

(
[
44
, Theorem 5.10]
)

Let
M
be a connected orientable compact surface distinct from
S^{2}
and
T^{2}
,
f\in{\mathcal{F}}(M,P)
, and
V\subset M
an
f
-adapted submanifold such that

(a)

each connected component of
V
is of dimension
\geq 1
, i.e. it is not a critical point of
f
;

(b)

V\not=\varnothing
whenever
M=D^{2}
or
S^{1}\times[0,1]
, so
\chi(M)<|V|
, whence
\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{id}}(M,V)
is always contractible, and we have an isomorphism

\pi_{1}\mathcal{O}_{f}(f,V)\xrightarrow[\cong]{\penalty\ \partial_{1}\penalty\ }\pi_{0}\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(f,V).

Then the sequence

(52)

{\mathbf{b}^{\prime}}\!\left(f,V\right):\pi_{0}\Delta^{\prime}(f,V)\lhook\joinrel\rightarrow\pi_{0}\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(f,V)\rightarrow\!\!\!\!\!\to\mathcal{G}^{\prime}(f,V)

belongs to the class
\mathcal{ZBP}
.

In particular,
\pi_{0}\Delta^{\prime}(f,V)
is a finitely generated free abelian group (which is already stated in Lemma
4.7.1
(3)
),
\pi_{1}\mathcal{O}_{f}(f,V)\cong\pi_{0}\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(f,V)
are solvable nearly Bieberbach group, and
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}(f,V)
is solvable and finite.

If
f\in\mathcal{M}^{smp}(M,P)
is a simple Morse map, then
{\mathbf{b}^{\prime}}\!\left(f,V\right)\in\mathcal{ZBP}_{2}
.

Notes to the proof.

Assume that
f\in\mathcal{F}(M,P)
has exactly
n
critical points.

1) Suppose
M
is a
2
-disk or a cylinder.
If
f
has no saddle critical points, then by Theorems
7.1.1
and
7.1.2
the sequence is either
\mathbf{z}_{0}
or
\mathbf{z}_{0}\wr\mathbf{z}_{1}
.
On the other hand, if
f
has critical point, then Theorem
7.2.1
shows that
\mathbf{b}^{\prime}(f,\partial M)
expresses via analogous sequences
\mathbf{b}^{\prime}(f|_{X_{i}},\partial X_{i})
and
\mathbf{b}^{\prime}(f|_{Y_{i}},\partial Y_{i})
by operations of direct products and wreath product
\cdot\wr\mathbf{z}_{m}
for some
m\geq 1
.
Since each
X_{i}
and
Y_{i}
is also a
2
-disk and cylinder and contain less number of critical points than
f
, one can apply the induction on
n
, and obtain that
\mathbf{b}^{\prime}(f,\partial M)
is obtained from a sequence
\mathbf{z}_{0}
by finitely many operations of direct products of short exact sequences and wreath products
\cdot\wr\mathbf{z}_{m}
for some
m
.

2) If
\chi(M)<0
, then by Theorem
6.1.1

\mathbf{b}^{\prime}(f,\partial M)
is again a product of sequences of the form
\mathbf{b}^{\prime}(f|_{B},\partial B)
, where
B
is either a
2
-disk or a cylinder, and therefore
\mathbf{b}^{\prime}(f,\partial M)
is also obtained from the sequence
\mathbf{z}_{0}
by finitely many operations of direct products of short exact sequences and wreath products
\cdot\wr\mathbf{z}_{m}
for some
m
.

If
f
is a generic Morse map, then an
f
-regular neighborhood of each critical leaf of
f
is a disk with two holes, which leads to the observation that in Theorem
7.2.1
we always have that
m=2
.
This implies the last statement of Theorem
7.3.4
.
∎

7.4.
Realization theorems for
{\mathbf{b}^{\prime}}\!\left(f,V\right)

We discuss here the question when an abstract group
G
can be realized as one of the groups (
52
) for some
(M,f,X)
.
The presented results are obtained in the papers S. Maksymenko and A. Kravchenko
[
21
]
, B. Feshchenko and A. Kravchenko
[
18
]
, I. Kuznietsova and Yu. Soroka
[
31
]
.

7.4.1.
Signs on the boundary

Let
M
be a compact surface.
Then by definition each
f\in\mathcal{F}(M,P)
takes constant values at connected components of
\partial M
.
Fix an orientation on
P
(which is
{\mathbb{R}}
or
S^{1}
).
Then for each boundary component
W
of
M
one can say whether
f
takes a local maximum or local minimum with respect to the orientation of
P
.
Hence one can associate to
f
a function
\varepsilon_{f}:\pi_{0}\partial M\to\{\pm 1\}
such that
\varepsilon_{f}(W)=-1
(resp
+1
) if
f
takes on
W
a local minimum (resp. maximum) with respect to the orientation of
P
.

For every function
\varepsilon:\pi_{0}\partial M\to\{\pm 1\}
define the following four spaces:

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{\varepsilon}(M,P)

\displaystyle=\{f\in\mathcal{F}(M,P)\mid\varepsilon_{f}=\varepsilon\},

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{\varepsilon}(M,P)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{M}(M,P)\cap\mathcal{F}_{\varepsilon}(M,P),

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}^{smp}_{\varepsilon}(M,P)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{M}^{smp}(M,P)\cap\mathcal{F}_{\varepsilon}(M,P),

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}^{gen}_{\varepsilon}(M,P)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{M}^{gen}(M,P)\cap\mathcal{F}_{\varepsilon}(M,P).

Also for every subspace
\mathcal{X}\subset\mathcal{F}(M,P)
consider the following sets of isomorphism classes of groups

\displaystyle\mathbf{S}_{\mathcal{X}}

\displaystyle=\{\pi_{0}\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(f,\partial M)\mid f\in\mathcal{X}\},

\displaystyle\mathbf{G}_{\mathcal{X}}

\displaystyle=\{\pi_{0}\mathcal{G}^{\prime}(f,\partial M)\mid f\in\mathcal{X}\},

and the following set of isomorphism classes of short exact sequences:

\mathbf{\bm{\Delta}SG}_{\mathcal{X}}:=\{{\mathbf{b}^{\prime}}\!\left(f,\partial M\right)\mid f\in\mathcal{X}\}.

Then we have the following relations between those classes:

\displaystyle\mathbf{S}_{\mathcal{M}^{gen}_{\varepsilon}(M,P)}\subset\mathcal{Z},

\displaystyle\mathbf{G}_{\mathcal{M}^{gen}_{\varepsilon}(M,P)}=\{1\},

\displaystyle\mathbf{S}_{\mathcal{M}^{smp}_{\varepsilon}(M,P)}\subset\mathcal{B}_{2},

\displaystyle\mathbf{G}_{\mathcal{M}^{smp}_{\varepsilon}(M,P)}\subset\mathcal{P}_{2},

\displaystyle\mathbf{S}_{\mathcal{M}_{\varepsilon}(M,P)}\subset\mathbf{S}_{\mathcal{F}_{\varepsilon}(M,P)}\subset\mathcal{B},

\displaystyle\mathbf{G}_{\mathcal{M}_{\varepsilon}(M,P)}\subset\mathbf{G}_{\mathcal{F}_{\varepsilon}(M,P)}\subset\mathcal{P}

\displaystyle\mathbf{\bm{\Delta}SG}_{\mathcal{M}^{gen}_{\varepsilon}(M,P)}\subset\mathcal{ZZI},

\displaystyle\mathbf{\bm{\Delta}SG}_{\mathcal{M}^{smp}_{\varepsilon}(M,P)}\subset\mathcal{ZBP}_{2},

\displaystyle\mathbf{\bm{\Delta}SG}_{\mathcal{M}_{\varepsilon}(M,P)}\subset\mathbf{\bm{\Delta}SG}_{\mathcal{F}_{\varepsilon}(M,P)}\subset\mathcal{ZBP}.

The inclusions for generic Morse maps follow from Lemma
4.5.1
, and all others from Theorem
7.3.4
.

Theorem 7.4.2
.

Let
M
be an orientable compact surface distinct from
T^{2}
and
S^{2}
, and
\varepsilon:\pi_{0}\partial M\to\{\pm 1\}
be any function.

(1)

If
M=S^{1}\times[0,1]
, and
\varepsilon
takes the same value on both connected components of
\partial M
, then

\displaystyle\ \ \ \mathbf{S}_{\mathcal{M}^{smp}_{\varepsilon}(M,P)}=\mathcal{B}_{2}\setminus\{1\},

\displaystyle\ \ \ \mathbf{S}_{\mathcal{M}_{\varepsilon}(M,P)}=\mathbf{S}_{\mathcal{F}_{\varepsilon}(M,P)}=\mathcal{B}\setminus\{1\},

\displaystyle\ \ \ \mathbf{G}_{\mathcal{M}^{smp}_{\varepsilon}(M,P)}=\mathcal{P}_{2},

\displaystyle\ \ \ \mathbf{G}_{\mathcal{M}_{\varepsilon}(M,P)}=\mathbf{G}_{\mathcal{F}_{\varepsilon}(M,P)}=\mathcal{P},

\displaystyle\ \ \ \mathbf{\bm{\Delta}SG}_{\mathcal{M}^{smp}_{\varepsilon}(M,P)}=\mathcal{ZBP}_{2}\setminus\{\mathbf{z}_{0}\},

\displaystyle\ \ \ \mathbf{\bm{\Delta}SG}_{\mathcal{M}_{\varepsilon}(M,P)}=\mathbf{\bm{\Delta}SG}_{\mathcal{F}_{\varepsilon}(M,P)}=

\displaystyle\ \quad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad=\mathcal{ZBP}\setminus\{\mathbf{z}_{0}\}.

In particular, for every
f\in\mathcal{F}_{\varepsilon}(M,P)
, the group
\pi_{0}\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(f,\partial M)
is always non-trivial.

(2)

In all other cases of
M
and
\varepsilon
we have that

\displaystyle\ \ \mathbf{S}_{\mathcal{M}^{smp}_{\varepsilon}(M,P)}=\mathcal{B}_{2},

\displaystyle\ \ \mathbf{S}_{\mathcal{M}_{\varepsilon}(M,P)}=\mathbf{S}_{\mathcal{F}_{\varepsilon}(M,P)}=\mathcal{B},

\displaystyle\ \ \mathbf{G}_{\mathcal{M}^{smp}_{\varepsilon}(M,P)}=\mathcal{P}_{2},

\displaystyle\ \ \mathbf{G}_{\mathcal{M}_{\varepsilon}(M,P)}=\mathbf{G}_{\mathcal{F}_{\varepsilon}(M,P)}=\mathcal{P},

\displaystyle\ \ \mathbf{\bm{\Delta}SG}_{\mathcal{M}^{smp}_{\varepsilon}(M,P)}=\mathcal{ZBP}_{2},

\displaystyle\ \ \mathbf{\bm{\Delta}SG}_{\mathcal{M}_{\varepsilon}(M,P)}=\mathbf{\bm{\Delta}SG}_{\mathcal{F}_{\varepsilon}(M,P)}=\mathcal{ZBP}.

Notes to the proof.

For groups
\mathbf{G}_{\mathcal{X}}
the proof is given in S. Maksymenko and A. Kravchenko
[
21
]
, and for groups
\mathbf{S}_{\mathcal{X}}
by I. Kuznietsova and Yu. Soroka
[
31
]
.
Notice that in the case
(1)
any map
f\in\mathcal{F}_{\varepsilon}(M,P)
has local minimum (or local maximum) on both boundary components of
M
.
Therefore
f
must have saddle critical points inside
M
, and therefore by Theorem
7.2.1
the group
\pi_{0}\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(f,\partial M)
is always non-trivial.
This explains why one should remove the unit group
\{1\}
from the classes in the first line of
(1)
.

A thorough analysis of arguments in
[
31
]
shows that they actually contain realization theorems for sequences
{\mathbf{b}^{\prime}}\!\left(f,\partial M\right)
.
This gives the corresponding relations for
\mathbf{\bm{\Delta}SG}_{\mathcal{X}}
.

The case
M=T^{2}
is described in Theorem
8.3.3
below.
∎

8.
Maps on
2
-torus

In this section we will describe the results of S. Maksymenko, B. Feshchenko, and A. Kravchenko

[
45
,
9
,
46
,
47
,
10
,
11
,
18
,
12
]

about algebraic structure of
\pi_{1}\mathcal{O}_{f}(f)
for the maps
f\in\mathcal{F}(T^{2},P)
.

Let
f\in\mathcal{F}(T^{2},P)
.
If
f
has no critical points, then
f
is a locally trivial fibration
T^{2}\to S^{1}
and the homotopy types of stabilizers and orbits for the pair
(f,\varnothing)
is described by Theorem
5.2.2
(D)
.

Assume that
f
has at least one critical point.
Then we have the following diagram (
41
) relating all the groups which we are interested in:

(53)

\displaystyle\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 157.71097pt\raise-42.80219pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ \mathcal{G}^{\prime}(f)\ }$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces

Recall that by Lemma
4.3.3
the graph
\Gamma_{f}
of
f
is either a tree or contains a unique cycle.
In each of those cases we will describe this diagram up to isomorphism.

Lemma 8.1
.

(
[
18
, Lemma 5.4]
)

Let
f\in\mathcal{F}(T^{2},P)
.
If
f
is either simple, or
P=S^{1}
and
f
is not null-homotopic, then
\Gamma_{f}
contains a cycle.

8.2.
Maps
f\in\mathcal{F}(T^{2},P)
whose graph
\Gamma_{f}
contains a cycle

Suppose
f:M\to P
be a map from
\mathcal{F}(T^{2},P)
such that its graph
\Gamma_{f}
contains a simple cycle
\gamma
.
Let
x
be a point belonging to some open edge of
\gamma
.
Then
x
corresponds to some regular leaf
C_{0}
of
f
.
Let

\mathcal{C}=\{h(C_{0})\mid h\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(f)\}

be the set of images of
C_{0}
under all maps of
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(f)
.
Then
\mathcal{C}
consists of finitely many, say
n
, leaves of
f
which can be cyclically ordered along
T^{2}
and enumerated as follows:

C_{0},\ C_{1},\ \cdots,\ C_{n-1}.

If
n\geq 2
, then for each
i
the leaves
C_{i}
and
C_{i+1}
bound a cylinder
Q_{i}
containing no other leaves
C_{j}
and
\mathrm{Int}{Q_{j}}\cap\mathrm{Int}{Q_{i}}=\varnothing
for
i\not=j
.
Every
h\in\mathcal{S}(f)
cyclically shift those cylinders.
Denote by
Q
one of them, e.g. put
Q=Q_{0}
.

Suppose
n=1
, so
C_{0}
is invariant with respect to
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(f)
, then
T^{2}\setminus C_{0}
is an open cylinder ‘‘bounded by
C_{0}
from both sides’’.
In this case we put
Q=\overline{T^{2}\setminus R_{C_{0}}}
, where
R_{C_{0}}
is any
f
-regular neighborhood of
C_{0}

Now we will define several constructions and homomorphisms.

a)

Recall that
T^{2}=S^{1}\times S^{1}
is a abelian group, and therefore for each
(a,b)\in T^{2}
, the shift
\eta_{a,b}:(x,y)\mapsto(x+a,y+b)
is a diffeomorphism of
T^{2}
.
Then the correspondence
(a,b)\mapsto\eta_{a,b}
is an embedding
\eta:T^{2}\lhook\joinrel\rightarrow\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{id}}(T^{2})
.
It is well known that
\eta
is a homotopy equivalence, see Table
1
.

b)

Let
C_{0}^{\prime}
be a simple closed curve which transversely intersects
C_{0}
at a unique point
x
.
It will be convenient to imagine
C_{0}^{\prime}
and
C_{0}
as a parallel and a meridian of
T^{2}
.
Let
\lambda=[C_{0}^{\prime}],\mu=[C_{0}]\in\pi_{1}(T^{2},x)
be the corresponding elements of the fundamental group of
T^{2}
.
Then one can assume that

\lambda=(1,0),\mu=(0,1)\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}\cong\pi_{1}T^{2}\xrightarrow[\cong]{\eta}\pi_{1}\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{id}}(T^{2}).

c)

Let
V_{0}
and
V_{1}
be the boundary components of
Q
.
By Corollary
7.2.2
one can represent
Q
as a ‘‘chain’’ of
n
cylinders
A_{1},\ldots,A_{n}
having the following properties.

•

V_{0}\subset\partial A_{1}
,
V_{1}\subset\partial A_{n}
, and only consecutive pairs
A_{i}
and
A_{i+1}
intersects and
A_{i}\cap A_{i+1}
is their common boundary circle;

•

\mathbf{b}^{\prime}(f|_{Q},\partial Q)\cong\prod\limits_{i=1}^{n}\mathbf{b}^{\prime}(f|_{A_{i}},\partial A_{i})
;

d)

Let
\varepsilon:\partial Q\to\{\pm 1\}
be the function defining the sings on the boundary of the restriction
f|_{Q}
, see Section
7.4.1
.
Thus
\varepsilon
takes constant values on each connected component
V
of
\partial Q
and equals
+1
(resp
-1
) if
f
takes on
V
a local maximum (resp. local minimum).
Then it is easy to see if
P={\mathbb{R}}
, then
\varepsilon
takes the same value on both boundary components of
\partial Q
, whence by Theorem
7.4.2
(1)
,
\pi_{0}\mathcal{S}(f|_{Q},\partial Q)
is a non-trivial group.
On the other hand, for the case
P=S^{1}
, the function
\varepsilon
takes distinct values on boundary components of
\partial Q
, whence in this case
\pi_{0}\mathcal{S}(f|_{Q},\partial Q)
can be trivial, which holds, e.g. when
f
has no critical points, see Theorem
5.2.2
(D)
.

e)

To simplify notations define the following groups
\mathcal{A}_{i}:=\pi_{0}\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(f|_{A_{i}},\partial A_{i})
,

\displaystyle\Delta_{\partial}

\displaystyle:=\pi_{0}\Delta^{\prime}(f|_{Q},\partial Q),

\displaystyle\Delta

\displaystyle:=\pi_{0}\Delta^{\prime}(f|_{Q}),

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{\partial}

\displaystyle:=\pi_{0}\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(f|_{Q},\partial Q),

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}

\displaystyle:=\pi_{0}\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(f|_{Q}).

Then the latter isomorphism for the sequence
\mathbf{b}^{\prime}(f|_{Q},\partial Q)
contains an isomorphism

\mathcal{S}_{\partial}\cong\mathop{\Pi}_{i=1}^{n}\mathcal{A}_{i}.

f)

Let
\widehat{\lambda}=([\mathrm{id}_{Q}],\ldots,[\mathrm{id}_{Q}],m)\in\mathcal{S}_{\partial}\mathop{\wr}\limits_{m}{\mathbb{Z}}
be the Garside element of the group
\mathcal{S}_{\partial}\mathop{\wr}\limits_{m}{\mathbb{Z}}
, see §
2.8
.
Then
\widehat{\lambda}
contained in the center of
\mathcal{S}_{\partial}\mathop{\wr}\limits_{m}{\mathbb{Z}}
.

g)

Let
\widehat{\mu}_{i}
be the Garside element of
\mathcal{A}_{i}
, and
\widehat{\mu}=(\widehat{\mu}_{1}\ldots,\widehat{\mu}_{n})
be the
diagonal Garside element
of
\mathcal{S}_{\partial}
, see (
15
).
Notice
\widehat{\mu}
is the generator of the kernel
j_{0}:\mathcal{S}_{\partial}\to\mathcal{S}
, and corresponds to a pair of Dehn twists along boundary components of
Q
produces in different directions.

h)

Recall that by Lemma
6.2.1
(ii)
there exists a section

\xi:\Delta\to\Delta_{\partial}\subset\mathcal{S}_{\partial}.

i)

Then one can define the following homomorphism:

\displaystyle\alpha:\pi_{1}\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{id}}(T^{2})\times\Delta^{m}\to\mathcal{S}_{\partial}\mathop{\wr}\limits_{m}{\mathbb{Z}},

\displaystyle\alpha(\lambda^{a}\mu^{b},q_{1},\ldots,q_{n})=\bigl(\xi(q_{1})\widehat{\mu}^{b},\ldots,\xi(q_{n})\widehat{\mu}^{b},am\bigr).

Since
\alpha(\lambda)=\widehat{\lambda}
and
\alpha(\mu)=(\widehat{\mu},\ldots,\widehat{\mu},0)
belongs to the center of
\mathcal{S}_{\partial}\mathop{\wr}\limits_{m}{\mathbb{Z}}
, one easily checks that
\alpha
is in fact a well-defined homomorphism.
Moreover, it is also easy to see that
\alpha
is injective.

Theorem 8.2.1
.

(
[
47
,
46
,
11
]
)

Diagram (
41
) is isomorphic to the following one:

\textstyle{\ \pi_{1}\mathcal{D}(T^{2})\oplus\bigl(\pi_{0}\Delta^{\prime}(f|_{Q})\bigr)^{m}\ \ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\alpha}
\scriptstyle{p_{1}}
\textstyle{\ \bigl(\pi_{0}\Delta^{\prime}(f|_{Q_{0}})\bigr)^{m}\ \ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\ \pi_{1}\mathcal{D}(T^{2})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ }
\scriptstyle{\alpha}
\textstyle{\ \pi_{0}\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(f|_{Q},\partial Q)\mathop{\wr}\limits_{m}{\mathbb{Z}}\ \ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\delta}
\textstyle{\ \pi_{0}\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(f|_{Q})\wr{\mathbb{Z}}_{m}\ \ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\ \mathcal{G}^{\prime}(f|_{Q})\wr{\mathbb{Z}}_{m}\ }

where
\delta(s_{1},\ldots,s_{m},k)=(j_{0}(s_{1}),\ldots,j_{0}(s_{m}),k\bmod m)
, and other arrows are obvious homomorphisms.

In particular, for the right columns of the corresponding diagrams we have the isomorphism, see (
13
):

\mathbf{b}^{\prime}(f)\cong\mathbf{b}^{\prime}(f|_{Q})\wr\mathbf{z}_{m}.

8.3.
Maps
f\in\mathcal{F}(T^{2},P)
whose graph
\Gamma_{f}
is a tree

Lemma 8.3.1
.

(
[
45
, Proposition 1]
)

Suppose
\Gamma_{f}
is a tree.
Then there exists a unique critical leaf
K
of
f
such that
T^{2}\setminus K
is a union of open
2
-disks.

It follows from uniqueness of such critical leaf, that
h(K)=K
for all
h\in\mathcal{S}(f)
.

Let
c=f(K)\in P
.
Take
\varepsilon>0
and consider the connected component
R_{K}
of
f^{-1}\bigl([c-\varepsilon,c+\varepsilon]\bigr)
containing
K
.
Decreasing
\varepsilon
one can assume that
R_{K}\cap\Sigma_{f}=K\cap\Sigma_{f}
.
Then
R_{K}
is an
f
-regular neighborhood of
K
, and it is also invariant with respect to
\mathcal{S}(f)
.

Similarly to §
7.2
let
\mathbf{Z}
be the collection of all connected components of
\overline{T^{2}\setminus R_{K}}
.
Then by Lemma
8.3.1
each element of
\mathbf{Z}
is a
2
-disk, and
\mathcal{S}(f)
interchanges those disks.
Let also

(54)

\mathcal{S}(\mathbf{Z})=\{h\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(f)\mid h(Z)=Z\ \text{for each}\ Z\in\mathbf{Z}\}

be the kernel of non-effectiveness of the action of
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(f)
on
\mathbf{Z}
.
Then the quotient
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(f)/\mathcal{S}(\mathbf{Z})

effectively
acts on
\mathbf{Z}
.

Theorem 8.3.2
.

(
[
45
,
9
,
10
,

,

]
)

(A)

Suppose all elements of
\mathbf{Z}
are invariant with respect to
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(f)
.
To simplify notation put

\begin{aligned} \ \Delta&:=\mathop{\Pi}\limits_{Z\in\mathbf{Z}}\pi_{0}\Delta^{\prime}(f|_{Z},\partial Z),&\ \mathcal{S}&:=\mathop{\Pi}\limits_{Z\in\mathbf{Z}}\pi_{0}\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(f|_{Z},\partial Z),&\ \mathcal{G}&:=\mathop{\Pi}\limits_{Z\in\mathbf{Z}}\mathcal{G}^{\prime}(f|_{Z},\partial Z).\end{aligned}

Then diagram (
41
) is isomorphic to the following one:

In particular, for the right column we have an isomorphism

(B)

Otherwise, the following statements hold.

a)

\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(f)/\mathcal{S}(\mathbf{Z})\cong{\mathbb{Z}}_{m}\oplus{\mathbb{Z}}_{n}
for some

;

on

is

.
For instance
\mathbf{Z}
contains

disks, where

is the number of orbits of that action.

Choose any collection

of elements of
\mathbf{Z}
belonging to mutually distinct orbits and for simplicity denote

\begin{aligned} \Delta&:=\mathop{\Pi}\limits_{i=1}^{c}\pi_{0}\Delta^{\prime}(f|_{Z_{i}},\partial Z_{i}),&\ \mathcal{S}&:=\mathop{\Pi}\limits_{i=1}^{c}\pi_{0}\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(f|_{Z_{i}},\partial Z_{i}),&\ \mathcal{G}&:=\mathop{\Pi}\limits_{i=1}^{c}\mathcal{G}^{\prime}(f|_{Z_{i}},\partial Z_{i}).\end{aligned}

) is isomorphic to the following one:

where
\alpha:\pi_{1}T^{2}\to\mathcal{S}\mathop{\wr}\limits_{m,n}{\mathbb{Z}}
is given by

\alpha(\lambda^{a}\mu^{b})=(e,\ldots,e,\,a\bmod m,\,b\bmod n)

and
e
is the unit of
\mathcal{S}
.

In particular, for the right column we have an isomorphism, see (
14
):

\mathbf{b}^{\prime}(f)\cong\Bigl(\prod_{Z\in\mathbf{Z}}\mathbf{b}^{\prime}(f|_{Z},\partial Z)\Bigr)\wr\mathbf{z}_{m,n}^{2}.

Evidently, (
55
) is a particular case of (
56
) for
m=n=1
.
The last identity can also be proved using Lemma
2.10.1
.

We will now formulate the results about the structure of
\mathbf{b}^{\prime}(f)
sequences for maps on
T^{2}
similar to
Theorems
7.3.4
and
7.4.2
.

Define the following spaces of maps:

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}^{\Psi}(T^{2},P)

\displaystyle=\{f\in\mathcal{F}(T^{2},P)\mid\text{$\Gamma_{f}$ is a tree}\},

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}^{O}(T^{2},P)

\displaystyle=\{f\in\mathcal{F}(T^{2},P)\mid\text{$\Gamma_{f}$ contains a cycle}\},

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}^{\Psi}(T^{2},P)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{M}(T^{2},P)\cap\mathcal{F}^{\Psi}(T^{2},P),

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}^{O}(T^{2},P)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{M}(T^{2},P)\cap\mathcal{F}^{O}(T^{2},P).

Then Lemma
8.1
implies that we have the following inclusions:

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}^{gen}(T^{2},P)\ \subset\ \mathcal{M}^{smp}(T^{2},P)\ \subset\ \mathcal{M}^{O}(T^{2},P)\ \subset\ \mathcal{F}^{O}(T^{2},P),

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}^{\Psi}(T^{2},P)\ \subset\ \mathcal{F}^{\Psi}(T^{2},P).

As a consequence of Theorems
7.4.2
,
8.2.1
,
8.3.2
we get the following:

Theorem 8.3.3
.

(
[
18
,
31
]
)

The following identities hold:

\displaystyle\mathbf{S}_{\mathcal{M}^{gen}(T^{2},P)}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{Z}=\{{\mathbb{Z}}^{n}\mid n=0,1,\ldots,\},

\displaystyle\mathbf{S}_{\mathcal{M}^{smp}(T^{2},{\mathbb{R}})}

\displaystyle=\{(A\times B)\mathop{\wr}\limits_{m}{\mathbb{Z}}\mid A,B\in\mathcal{B}_{2}\setminus\{1\},m\geq 1\},

\displaystyle\mathbf{S}_{\mathcal{M}^{smp}(T^{2},S^{1})}

\displaystyle=\{A\mathop{\wr}\limits_{m}{\mathbb{Z}}\mid A\in\mathcal{B}_{2},m\geq 1\},

\displaystyle\mathbf{S}_{\mathcal{M}^{O}(T^{2},{\mathbb{R}})}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{S}_{\mathcal{F}^{O}(T^{2},{\mathbb{R}})}=\{(A\times B)\mathop{\wr}\limits_{m}{\mathbb{Z}}\mid A,B\in\mathcal{B}\setminus\{1\},m\geq 1\},

\displaystyle\mathbf{S}_{\mathcal{M}^{O}(T^{2},S^{1})}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{S}_{\mathcal{F}^{O}(T^{2},S^{1})}=\mathcal{B},

\displaystyle\mathbf{S}_{\mathcal{M}^{\Psi}(T^{2},P)}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{S}_{\mathcal{F}^{\Psi}(T^{2},P)}=\{A\mathop{\wr}\limits_{m,n}{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}\mid A\in\mathcal{B},m,n\geq 1\},

\displaystyle\mathbf{G}_{\mathcal{M}^{gen}(T^{2},P)}

\displaystyle=\{1\},

\displaystyle\mathbf{G}_{\mathcal{M}^{smp}(T^{2},P)}

\displaystyle=\{G\wr{\mathbb{Z}}_{m}\mid\text{for some $G\in\mathcal{P}_{2}$ and $m\geq 1$}\},

\displaystyle\mathbf{G}_{\mathcal{M}^{O}(T^{2},P)}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{G}_{\mathcal{F}^{O}(T^{2},P)}=\mathcal{P},

\displaystyle\mathbf{G}_{\mathcal{M}^{\Psi}(T^{2},P)}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{G}_{\mathcal{F}^{\Psi}(T^{2},P)}=\{G\wr({\mathbb{Z}}_{m}\times{\mathbb{Z}}_{n})\mid G\in\mathcal{P},m,n\geq 1\},

\displaystyle\mathbf{\bm{\Delta}SG}_{\mathcal{M}^{gen}(T^{2},P)}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{ZZI},

\displaystyle\mathbf{\bm{\Delta}SG}_{\mathcal{M}^{smp}(T^{2},{\mathbb{R}})}

\displaystyle=\{(\mathbf{u}\times\mathbf{v})\wr\mathbf{z}_{m}\mid\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}\in\mathcal{ZBP}_{2}\setminus\{\mathbf{z}_{0}\},m\geq 1\},

\displaystyle\mathbf{\bm{\Delta}SG}_{\mathcal{M}^{smp}(T^{2},S^{1})}

\displaystyle=\{\mathbf{u}\wr\mathbf{z}_{m}\mid\mathbf{u}\in\mathcal{ZBP},m\geq 1\},

\displaystyle\mathbf{\bm{\Delta}SG}_{\mathcal{M}^{O}(T^{2},P)}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{\bm{\Delta}SG}_{\mathcal{F}^{O}(T^{2},P)}=

\displaystyle\qquad=\{(\mathbf{u}\times\mathbf{v})\wr\mathbf{z}_{m}\mid\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}\in\mathcal{ZBP}\setminus\{\mathbf{z}_{0}\},m\geq 1\},

\displaystyle\mathbf{\bm{\Delta}SG}_{\mathcal{M}^{\Psi}(T^{2},P)}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{\bm{\Delta}SG}_{\mathcal{F}^{\Psi}(T^{2},P)}=

\displaystyle\qquad=\{\mathbf{u}\wr\mathbf{z}_{m,n}^{2}\mid\mathbf{u}\in\mathcal{ZBP},m,n\geq 1\}.

The proof for classes
\mathbf{G}_{\mathcal{X}}
is given in
[
18
]
and for
\mathbf{S}_{\mathcal{X}}
in
[
31
]
.
Those proofs imply realization theorems for
\mathbf{\bm{\Delta}SG}_{\mathcal{X}}
.

Partial computation of sequences
\mathbf{b}^{\prime}(f)
were obtained in
[
19
,
20
]
and in
[
29
]
for the cases when
M
is
2
-sphere and Möbius band respectively.
They are not complete, and therefore we do not present them here.

9.
First homology groups of orbits

Let
M
be a connected compact orientable surface distinct from
S^{2}
, and
f\in\mathcal{F}(M,P)
.
Let also

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}^{\prime}=\mathbf{S}_{\mathcal{F}^{\Psi}(T^{2},P)}=\{A\mathop{\wr}\limits_{m,n}{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}\mid A\in\mathcal{B},m,n\geq 1\}.

Then by Theorems
8.3.3
and
7.4.2
the fundamental group
G=\pi_{1}\mathcal{O}_{f}(f)
belongs either to
\mathcal{B}
or to
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
.
Moreover, as mentioned after definitions class
\mathcal{B}
in Sections
7.3.1
,
G
is obtained from the unit group by finitely many operations of direct products and wreath products of the form
\cdot\mathop{\wr}\limits_{m}{\mathbb{Z}}
, and (for the case when
M=T^{2}
and
\Gamma_{f}
is a tree) possibly
a unique and the last
operation of wreath product of the form
\cdot\mathop{\wr}\limits_{a,b}{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}
for some
a,b\geq 1
.
One can formalize this observation as follows.

Consider the following two alphabets:

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}

\displaystyle=\bigl\{\ 1,\,{\mathbb{Z}},\,(,\,),\,\times\ \bigr\}\ \cup\ \bigl\{\ \mathop{\wr}\limits_{a}{\mathbb{Z}}\ \bigr\}_{a\geq 1}

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{A}\ \cup\ \bigl\{\ \mathop{\wr}\limits_{a,b}{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}\ \bigr\}_{a,b\geq 1},

so
\mathcal{A}
it consists of the unit group
1
, group on integers
{\mathbb{Z}}
, brackets ‘‘(’’ and ‘‘)’’, a product sign ‘‘
\times
’’, and
wreath product with
{\mathbb{Z}}
symbols ‘‘
\mathop{\wr}\limits_{a}{\mathbb{Z}}
’’ for all
a\geq 1
, while
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
additionally contains
wreath products with
{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}
symbols ‘‘
\mathop{\wr}\limits_{a,b}{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}
’’ for all
a,b\geq 1
.

Then every group
G\in\mathcal{B}
(resp.
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
) is written (though not in a unique way) as a word
w
in the alphabet
\mathcal{A}
(resp.
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
).
For example,
{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}
can be written by the following words:

\displaystyle{\mathbb{Z}}\times{\mathbb{Z}},

\displaystyle{\mathbb{Z}}\times(1\mathop{\wr}\limits_{a}{\mathbb{Z}}),

\displaystyle 1\times(1\mathop{\wr}\limits_{a}{\mathbb{Z}})\times(1\mathop{\wr}\limits_{b}{\mathbb{Z}}),

\displaystyle 1\mathop{\wr}\limits_{a,b}({\mathbb{Z}}\times{\mathbb{Z}}).

for any
a,b\geq 1
.
Of course, there are words which do not define a group, e.g.
){\mathbb{Z}}\mathop{\wr}\limits_{4}
.

Given a group
G\in\mathcal{B}
(resp.
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
) every word
w
which correctly defines
G
will be called a
realization of
G
in the alphabet
\mathcal{A}
(resp.
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
)
.
Denote by
\beta_{1}(w)
the number of symbols
{\mathbb{Z}}
in the word
w
.

The following result is obtained by I. Kuznietsova and Yu. Soroka as a consequence of Lemma
2.6.1
.

Theorem 9.1

(
[
31
]
)
.

Let
G\in\mathcal{B}
(resp.
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
), and
w
be any realization of
G
in the alphabet
\mathcal{A}
(resp.
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
).
Then the center and the abelianization of
G
are free abelian groups of the same rank
\beta_{1}(w)
:

Z(G)\cong G/[G,G]\cong{\mathbb{Z}}^{\beta_{1}(w)}.

In particular,
\beta_{1}(w)
does not depend on a concrete realization
w
of
G
in the alphabet
\mathcal{A}
(resp.
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
).

Recall that by Hurewicz theorem, e.g.
[
15
, Theorem 2A.1]
, for any path connected topological space
X
with the fundamental group
G=\pi_{1}X
we have an isomorphism
H_{1}(X,{\mathbb{Z}})\cong G/[G,G]
.
Hence we get the following

Corollary 9.2

(
[
31
]
)
.

Let
M
be a connected compact orientable surface distinct from
S^{2}
,
f\in\mathcal{F}(M,P)
,
G=\pi_{1}\mathcal{O}_{f}(f)
, and
\beta_{1}
be the number of symbols
{\mathbb{Z}}
in any realization of
G
in the alphabet
\mathcal{A}
(or
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
).
Then the first homology group
H_{1}(\mathcal{O}_{f}(f),{\mathbb{Z}})\cong G/[G,G]
is a free abelian group of rank
\beta_{1}
, that is
\beta_{1}
is the first Betti number of the orbit
\mathcal{O}_{f}(f)
.
